I " Where—in what place will the lady speak— I mean, will it be in the church ?" ventured Miss Philura in a depressed whisper. She sighed ap- prehensively as she glanced down at the tips of . her shabby gloves. Idly." A majestic and inclusive sweep of a per. fectly gloved hand suggested infinity and reduced Miss Philura into shrinking silence. When Mrs. B. WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Courts.—The orficial announcement regarding the holding of the Courts at Buckingham Palace heralds the approach of the London season, which in view of the opening of a new Parliament, promises a very brilliant beginning. There will be two Courts next month, and two after Easter, and this year a great many debutantes are waiting to make their curtsey to their Sovereign. As a rule these young people like to get their presenta- tion over at one of the earlier Courts. It is something of an ordeal to the youthful debutante to traverse the whole length of the Throne room, for the moment, the sole object of interest to many hundreds of people present, but they become early in the year eligible for Court and Society invitations, and there are few girls who do not look forward and indulge in day dreams over there first season.

Some of the Prosenta. tions.—PrincessEna of Batten- burg made her (taut last year, and there will be no royal chibutante this season. Princess, Louise, the daughter of Prince and Princess Louis of Battenberg will not he seventeen until next July, and the only other Princess approaching eligible age is Princess Alexandra, daughter of the Princess Royal, Duchess of Fife, who will be fifteen in May. Notable among the young girls who will appear at the Courts are Lady Violet Manners, granddaughter of the Duke of Rutland, Lady Jean Cochrane, daughter of Lady Dundonald, and Miss Joan Campbell, niece of the Duke of Argyll, while among the brides will be Lady Brite, Lady Loch, and Lady Lewisham. The two ladies Mrs. Haig and Mrs., Bell—who were Miss Vivian, and Miss Mary Dyke, Maids of Honour to the Queen—will also be among the youthful matrons to be presented on their marriage. King Alfonso's Marriage.—Prineess Henry of Batten- •berg, and her daughter Princess Ena, are staying at Biarritz, on a visit to Princess Frederica of Hanover, who has a charming villa there. It is a French water- ing place, a few miles from the Spanish frontier, and the ex-Empress Eugenie lived at Biarritz for many years prior to her marriage in 1853. It was then an insignificant village, but it is now a fashionable winter resort, and, if reports are all believed, the King of Spain is about to make a short stay there. If that is so, we may expect an authoritative announcement of his engagement with Princess Ens, which has been the subject of many rumors, but appears only now to await the conclusion of the necessary formb.lities. Don Alfonso has announced his intentioe of being a modern king, but one can readily understand that the traditional etiquette, and somewhat, out-of-date grandeur which reigns at the Spanish Court, involves the most elabor- ate and ceremonious arrangements in connection with his forthcoming marriage. The German Silver Wedding.—The German Emperor and Empress will next mouth celebrate their silver wedding and the occasion will be marked by great festivities at the German Court. The Emneror is a little younger than his wife, Augusta Victoria, who is forty-seven, and for over seventeen years of his married life, he has been on the throne. The family of the Imperial couple consists of the Crown Prince, and five other sons, and an only daughter, whilst the Empress has always been spoken of as an ideal housewife, and enjoys the reputation of a perfect materfarnilias being devoted to husband and children. The Kaiser seems still to be a little offended with his uncle King Edward as he has never returned the visit which his Majesty paid him at Kiel, eighteen months ago, and matters do not appear to have changed since last summer, when King Edward rather pointedly kept aloof from his nephew whilst staying in German soil at Marienbad. The Noise of London.—ln the old days before people had begun to cultivate nerves, the Londoner was rather : proud of the roar and noise of his great city. It was that which filled the country visitor with awe, and, if he or she were not duly impressed there was some dis- appointment. Now there are complaints on all sides that the noise of the traffic, and street cries is fast making the metropolis impossible, as a place of resid- ence, and one authority declares that the noise of London kills off a London family in the third genera- tion. :Many suggestions are being offered to mitigate the evil, and some steps are being taken to this end, but much remains to be done before London will com- pare equally in this respect with most other capitals in Europe._

===== 0002601_19060120_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
hand-painted long evening glove, has "caught on " here. Only the arm portion is decorated in this way, and the 'lowers and foliage so portrayed should to be correct, match those in evidence upon the gown. Need- less to say the notion is an expensive one, unless the home artist can manage a spray of moss roses, forget- me-nots, violets, or some other suitable design herself. Then when the hand portion is soiled, the best plan is to cut it off at the wrist, match the arm portion exactly in three-button length gloves, and stitch neatly together. %Veit done, the join will not be noticeable, especially if a bracelet or two is worn, and thus one pair of" arras "can be made to do duty over and over again.

WARRINGTON CO-OPERATIVE

SOCIETY.

Annual Meeting.

The annual meetina of the cVarrington Co-operative Society was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey- street, on Tuesday evening, Mr. T. Critchley presiding over a good attendance. TICE YEAR'S PROGRESS. The CHAIRMAN said the committee had the greatest pleasure in again being able to present a satisfactory quarterly balance sheet. The total sales amounted to £39,862, an increase of £2,632 over the corresponding quarter of last year. During the past year the sales bad totalled £151,031 as compared with ..£144,262 in 1904 and an increase of £18,411 over the sales in 1903, Div idends•paid in 1905 amounted to 224,794 as compared with £23,654 the previous year. lie thought they had every reason to be satisfied with the excellent progress which they were making. (Applause.) ELECTIONS.

Mr.FARBAR proposed the re-election of Mr.Critchley as President of the Society. The proposition was heartily carried. Nominations were received to fill four vacancies on the committee, caused by the retirement of Messrs. Timperley, Hall and Bibby, and the elevation to the presidency of Mr. Critchley. In proposing the name of Mr. Owen one member said that in the past it had been a matter of consider- able difficulty to get on the committee in consequence of the constant re-election of old members. He had the greatest respect for the old committee men and for the lwork which they did but he thought it advisable that they should introduce a certain ai...iount of new blood every time vacancies occurred. (Hear, hear.) The voting resulted as follows :—Tiraperlov 3?„ Hall . 32, Bibby 24, W. Haigh 23, Owen 20, flasht6n 17, and Clare 13, the first four named being elected. Mr. J. Green Chappell was unanimously re-elected lauditor.

WARRINGTON ANGLERS" ASSOCIATION.

Annual Meeting.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Anglers' Association was held at the Norton Arms Hotel on Friday evening. There was a fair attendance of mem- 'hers the meeting being under the presidency of Mr. NV. A. Proud.

AN UNSATISFACTORY SEASON. Mr. P. KNIGHT (secretary), in his 15th annual report, said the state of the association Was fully maintained both numerically and financially, though the arrears of subscriptions was far in excess of the previous year. In future any person in arears would be required to clear them off and pay a deposit for the current year before being supplied with a new card. The system of secretaries of clubs collecting the association fees had again worked satisfactorily on the whole, and as a means of encouraging it had been decided that in future 5 per cent. commission would be returned to the club whose secretary collected and paid in .11,10 whole of the subscriptions before June 30th. 'taking angling all the season through it bad been a very disappointing one, especially after the rainy season set in in July, all waters suffering. Many favourite waters that had for years yielded good all round sport had been litter failures. Stocking had again been exten- sively carried ontAnd during the past three years 25,000 fish had been placed in the Bridgewater Canal, which, he ventured to assert,was a record for an association of a size similar to their own. The angling contests of the year had been an unqualified success, and reflected no little credit on the association. The amounts realized from entrance fees and the grants from the Association funds together with the specimen prizes, represented an r"--aggregate of £63 15s. distributed among 123 prize winners, which averaged lls. 2d. per prize. The specimen list showed a little falling off in the number of fish weighed in for competition, but the quality was somewhat in advance of last year. Reverting to the defaulter's list, it was amazing to find that they had been unable to conclude the year so satisfactorily, in view of the tact that there were no fewer than 148 members who were deficient in their payments to the extent of .£3O. That was an alarming state of things, and he hoped the secretaries of all ellibs would be watchful in not admitting to membership' any person whose name appeared on the defaulter's list. They themselves intended to make all the remedial measures possible,which be hoped would be effective. The balance sheet showed a balance in hand Of 27 es. 31 ELECTIONS. Mr. E. M. Crosfleld was unanimously re-elected presi- dent. The following vice-presidents were elected : The Rev. W. Bracecamp, Colonel Ridgway and Messrs. A. H. Crosfleld, J. C. Parr Lin. Greening, J. Lyon Whittle, W. H. Podmore, %V. Brown, W. it. Proud, B. Kenyon, P. T. Bennie, A. Taylor, E. Pickavance, T. .1. Garnett, T. Gardner, J. IV. Wright, A. Whittaker, F. J. Grocott, J. Critchley, Harry Taylor, F. J. Emson. T. Starkey. W. C. Tetlow, E. Caldwell jun., Alfred Shaw, Aquila Cook, J. Podmore, sen. J. Waywell, F. Bonnell and J. Taylor.

COMMITTER. VIVA members of the committee retired, and the Ica- lowine were elected to fill the v..cancies. Messrs. F. Bonnell, T. Boseow, G. Smith, S. R. Lodge and J. Hackney. Votes of thanks to the officers for their services con- 'eluded the meeting.

LATHAM'S CAPTIVE.

Dv DORA L ANGLOTS, Author of "The Kiss of Judas," "A Family Failing," 'ix,

The mail coach was half-way between Sawbricl: Edge and Thorn Bottom, the time well on in the afternoon, when the Californian roads are glaring and the red dust as itching and irritating as a plaister of cantharides. On the bos-seat by the driver sat a man in an airy blue shirt. and a wide- awake hat, irreproachable in its unsunned fresb- ness ; behind and above him was his colleague and superior, the superintendent, who had brought. him down from headquarters to go over the books of a certain local postmaster suspected of having con- tracted a malady likely to lead to his forgetting what he had done with certain moneys lately entrusted to his care. On the' other side of Tarbuck the driver, sat Judge Crupp, the wrinkles in his face outlined by the dust till his countenance resembled a map done in red ink. As the former tooled his cattle past the bend of the junction road a youth on a sorrel mare joined the track, and suiting his pace to theirs, kept just sufficiently behind them to be on the edge of the dust-cloud raieed by their transit.

James W. Tarbuck was not an emotional man for any perceptible slope in his cold grey eyes, his &ice might never have left his leaders' ears, never have taken in the figure of the solitary traveller at all. That glance was still on the red I tassels dangling from the fly-nets when he demanded laconically: "Maybe, jedge, and you, Mr. Latham. never heard tell how rick-purse and his pard held up Dyneford's mail a fortnight since? " Both gentlemen having intimated their profound ignorance of the details of that, interesting exploit, Tarbuck, having transferred his quid to his left cheek, and astonished an inoffensive tree-lizard with an unexpected baptism of tobacco-juice, pro- ceeded thus :

"They was spankin' along—much as we might be now—when a young stranger on a bey hoss—or it mitget be a sorrel mare—jines the coach-road and passes the time of day. Things goes on for half an hour in an or'nary kind of way, till they gets into a holler, and thar they 'finds Peck-purse awaitin' for them. What toilers? Why, he holds up the man in front, while his pard goes through the inside passengers. That's so, gents. Happen neither of you has got your shootin'-iron handy ? " Latham and Judge Crupp exchanged glances. There was no need for any lengthy explanation; on a coach-road, with "agents" known to be out, there could be but one point to such a story. "No hurry, gents," remarked Tarbuck, who had observed the judge's hand quietly seeking his hip- pocket. "We're on a incline now; when I take the brake off, you, jedge, might ,draw on him ; Mr. Lathant here Will do the rest. I shall pull up when we reach the level."

The incline was steep: the horses had-their hind legs under their belly-bands ; the grit between the wheels and the brake gave out a sound like the whirr of a grindstone, deadening the pad of the hoofs behind.

As the coach reached the bottom the judge faced round in his seat, and the sun glinted on the barrel of his six-shooter.

"Hold 'en up! I reckon, stranger, we want a talk with yon."

The young man dropped his reins on the mare's neck, and, being still on the incline, pitched forward a little as her head went down ; but, • steadying himself with his knee-grip, threw his hands above his head. The coach was at a standstill. Latham had already swung himself from the wheel to the ground, and was picking his way back through the subsiding dust to the sorrel mare and its rider. Ire happened to have a half-smoked cheroot in his mouth, and had not thought it necessary to discard it for such a simple piece of work as that which lay before him.

"Come off!" he commanded, having ascertained first that the mare's saddle was not furnished with holsters. "You can grip the pommel with your left hand;. and don't be as slow as Molasses in January!"

The stranger, a handsome youth with dark curls hanging on his damp forehead, came off awkwardly enough, and stood up facing his captor, between him and the twinkling fore-shortened barrel on the coach top. Straight as a die he stood, with his hands raised—hands that were rapidly whitening as the blood left them and flowed back upon his heart. Cigar in mouth, the smoke of it uncivilly curling up into the stranger's nostrils, Latham set about his work.

No weapon in the hip-pocket, nor in the buff belt, and none concealed in the riding-boot. Not so much as It bowie even! Latham was puzzled. Straightening himself up, and using both hands simultaneously, he passed them downwards from the stranger's armpits to his belt. ' The heat of the sun had possibly something to do with the sudden flush that had mantled to his brow, lie drew away and met his captive's eye.

"Where's your weapun ? Tell me, and take it quietly."

'lln n-ly bre:ult.'.

"Well, it's no fa,..t of mine; I've got to have Latham's brow Nvas a rich copper-bronze as he plunged his hand into the youth's shirt and drew out from that unusual resting-place a little toy revolver.

"Anything else in this line?" Latham had dropped his cigar and put his foot on it. "No—honour bright." "All right. I'll chance It.* You will not--" "No; there's no need that I ran see. YOU malt rely on me." Latham turned to the coach onc; more, from which the short-5ig:,1,1 superintendent wag blinking at Plient over the jacii4e s shoulder. "Put up your iron, judge I've drawn his teeth," he shouted. "Now, 'fatback, what's it to be ? "

Tarbnck's dry ,voire framed the answer. ''Ask the 'stranger to step inside, and hitch the mare to the back of the coach. The company won't charge him a red cent for the ride. One of the gents will take his shooter and if wc ..00ttld meet anything onpleasnnt on the rond—

"I'll see this through thyself," Latham inter- rupted ; "that is, if any gentleman will oblige by swapping seats with me? " There arpeared to be no difficulty in the matter. All fiv..4 of the perspiring travellers were eager to do the civil thing, but, as only one outside seat was on the market, the privilege was at la§t unani- mously conceded to a gentleman who wore woollen socks, and had a fancy for travelling with his shoes kicked off. After he had tumbled out Latham stood back and allowed his charge to enter the vehicle.

"I'm depending on you, Mr. Latham," Tarbuck Shouted back as he gathered his reins once more.

i your man," Latham answered, busying himself with the mare's rein, and carefully avoiding ..his captive's eyes. "I reckon Pick-purse is euchred this time," said Tarbnek, as the coach lurched forward. •"When I get rid of that box of bullion down to the office at Thorn Bottom, the youngster can go to the dence.!' In the reeking interior of the coach Latham sat and sweated opposite his hostage. Yes, that was what the youth was—a hostage. True, he might have nothing whatever to do with the "road agent, euphemistically styled Pick-purse, nor with any other of the breed ; yet there he sat, acquiescing in the position by his very silence, as though it were not tm undeserved fate, btit merely one of the chances of the road that had befallen him. No ioubt he understood himself that his life mu

===== 0002601_19060120_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner,

am _ for the safety of the persons and personal belongings of his fellow-travellers, for shoot first and ask questions after is the order of things on the road. His fate was in Latham's hands,, yet somehow the man felt powerless to exert his free will in the matter. He knew that he had only to shew the least disinclination to act on any emergency that might arise to have his prisoner pass from his keeping to that of the other four.

'ewer

• . It was not the first time that Latham had travelled the mountain roads, yet he had never before felt a nerve quiver at the thought of molesta- tion. Now every sense in him seemed strained to attention he even found himself involuntarily attempting a voiceless prayer that there might be no danger ahead. He was not aware that he had gone so far till the unusual nature of the exercise pulled him up short, with the conviction that he could not get through with it. Twilight was falling; the coach had nearly reached its destination for the night. The halt was always made at Abernethy's saloon. There were no mlays there ; the same team and the same driver would start for Bluff's Gap the following morning. Perhaps the fact that the coach proprietor and the saloon-keeper were brothers had something to do with the arrangement. As the little embryo town came in sight it wris the superintendent who leant forward and remarked : "Say, judge! About lettin that young man loose when Nft strike Abernethy's ? Take a squint at this." Here he produced a week-old copy of the Red-pallt _Hever. "It was a man en a sorrel mare that held hp Dyneford's lot. There are people at the Gap that could identify. Me and Latham are agoin' on. We've got the youth; what say if we keep him ? " So it chanced that when Latham alighted from the coach like a man new made, and sang out: "I'm going to ask this gentleman what he'll take to drink, and shall be happy if you'll all join us," 'Judge Crupp, after naming his particular "poison," whispered a few words in his ear that made him swallow his liquor as if he needed it. "This is yours, Mr. Latham ; you share it with this gent. It's the best we can do, being full up."

"All right." Latham shut the door and turned the key in the lock. His captive, who was sitting on one of the camp-beds With his head in his hands, sprang up. "What's that for? " he demanded breathlessly. "Can't you guess?" was the reply. "I didn't bargain for this," stammered the youth. "It seems they suspect something. They've taken my horse, you have my revolver, and if I'm bound to stay here, stay I must ; • • -

"Half a minute," Latham interrupted. "I'm acting on the square in this ; its none of my seeking. If you're not clear on that point say the word, and I'll back out of it, and leave the business to some other man."

"My God! no! But what does it all mean I How did they discover--"

" Hush ! That's enough. You're opening your head too wide. They ve discovered nothing, but they don't mean to lose sight of you till we get to the next stage, where there are Government agents who might identify." "I'm not known to any of them" "So much the better. But Judge Crupp says there are men there who know Pick-purse and his young pal by sight." "They take me for a road agent?" "Why, certainly." "It's false. I've nothing whatever to do with that lot." Latham had sunk jute a canvas-backed chair, and was mopping his damp brow convulsively. "Then what in thunder is your game? " he gasped. The captive had crossed the room, and was down on the floor at Latham's knees.

"Mr. Latham, I am as innocent of this charge as a babe. For God's sake think—think of the position and get me out of this." "There's only one way to satisfy them," he answered, turning his head away. "Make a clean breast of it, and throw yourself on their mercy." "What—now? After being locked up here alone with you! It's impossible. That alone would prevent it, if I had no other reason." Latham moved uneasily in his seat, but answered nothing. "As God's my Judge, my mission holds no wrong to any man, but it is urgent—most urgent. Let me go. If ever you have been in a sore strait, and by the help of Providence got out of it, think of your escape, and pay your debt to God."

The captive's eyes were swimming in tears, the captive's hand lay trembling on his wrist. "Give me my weapon, and let ale go." "I'll be the butt of the whole crowd all my life if I let you fool me."

"A laugh—a laugh amainst a man. What's that. compared with--"

"Hush! The Lord Harry knows who's about, and the place is nothing but lath and plaster." The voices of captor and captive dropped now to whispers. Five minutes later Latham unlocked his door and shouted down the passage for two whiskey • cock-tails.

About the middle of the night there was a little bustle in the stable-yard and the crack of a revolver. Men tumbled out to inquire the cause, and learnt that someone had procured a horse and cleared out, followed by a leaden benison from the sleepy ostler. A rush was DIA& for each man's personal belongings. Nothing the stranger's small valise. Then the whole crowd gathered by common consent. at Lat ham's door. It was locked, and all was quiet within. Getting no response to repeated calls, they broke into the room, and found him alone, lying back in the canvas chair fast asleep.

At his elbow stood the tray and glasses. One was untouched, its contents fiat as ditch-water, not a sparkle in the aromatic ruby depths. The dregs in the other were not only flat but muddy also. "The man who mixed that cock-tail had a tarna- tion queer receipt," remarked Judge Crupp, as he set the glass down after lifting it to his nose. As no one had lost a gold watch or a dollar bill, there was no pursuit. It is °Le thing to press for a young man's company, and quite another to go riding after him into the night, with a prospect of dropping into a hornet's nest. For a man who had swallowed half a bottle of his own toothache-drops, Latham looked famous the following morning, and quite fit, as he himself declared, to push on and fix up those accounts. Out of respect for his gastronomic qualities there was very little chafing about the episode. A man may swallow chloroform and yet not be able to stomach personal remarks.

There was no incident to mark the next day's journey. The coach deposited Latham and the superintendent safely at their destination, and, having liquored up, they crossed the public square at 5 p. In., and entered the local post-office, where a girl behind the counter was sorting the newly. arrived mails.

"My brother is ont, gentlemen," she said, with- out looking up. "What cau Ido for you?" The superintendent explained their object, though not their suspicions, to her. "I guess you'd better start at once," she said. "I'll help you all I know when I am through with these mails. My brother has had a touch of the sun lately, and the work is a bit behind, but I think you'll find things pretty square. There are the books PH get you the key of the safe." Short46..ted as he was, the superintendent had an eye for beauty, and his gaze followed the girl's comely figure as she retired into a little Mlle! office. As for Latham, he was standing back in the shadow, nervously chewing his tawny moustache. Not a muscle of hers quivered when she handed him the key, though he was red and white in turns, and had burst into a sweat ; but then, of course, he had come upon her suddenly—she had probably seen him crossing the square. _

„ He thought she looked half-defiantly at him. He was glad of that ; to succumb to a convenient fit of drowsiness is one thing, to cook accounts another, and a wan never knows what he may cl,] till he is asked.

It was midnight before the two were through with their work, and the postmaster was still absent. Latham shut the last book with a trium- phant slap, and remarketl. '

"Well, boss, there are irregularities, but not one red cent gone a-missing that I can trace."

"Right you are," rejoined the superintendent. "Come on, and m• shall have time for a game of poker with the hove." • _

Latham consented .;o the proposal, but half-way across the square he ',membered that he had for- gotten something, and etraced his steps. The official entrance to the post-office being closed, he made his way to the private entrance, giving on a little passage; it was on the latch, and pushing it open he entered.

STRANGE RESULT OF A CHILL. An Oddfellow's " Annual " Skin Disease Ended by Zam-Buk.

One peculiar result of a cold or chill is often an un- sightly and torturing rash. Mr. Edward James Goldstone, of 39, Garnet Street. Bedminster, Bristol, who is a sick visitor of the Odd- fellows F.S., has given the history of his cure by Zam- Bak to a • Bristol Times and Mirror " reporter. "Neatly twenty sears ago," he said, "I caught a chill and it left me with a rash all over my head and body. I bad it three or four months, and then it got better, but at thesame time the following year it appeared twain. 1 went to doctors, and they prescribed for me, but it got very little better, so I went to the General Hospital. After a few weeks they discharged me as cured,. but I was far from cured, for every year since I have had the rash all over my head at regular intervals. I am a sick pay visitor for the Oddfellows' Friendly Society, and I used to feel ashamed to perform my duties. I tried medicines of all kinds until I was tried of them. This year I saw Zam-Buk advertised, and gave the balm a trial. I followed the proprietors' hints ,as to diet, and the first box did me a vast amount of good. Whenever I applied Zarn-Buk the sensation was very soothing, and I felt it was benefiting me. I obtained another box, and continuing the treatment was perfectly cured. I decided to be convinced Zam-Buk had ended the disease, so I have waited for some time. There has been no return of the rash since it was cured by Zarn-Bak, as was always the case when I was cured 'by other means, and lam now satisfied that I am permanentlyfreed from the trouble." Zam-Buk is most soothing and curative for skin troubles that are aggravated or caused by the cold weather. NORTHERN UNION LEAGUE.

RESULTS OF SATURDAY'S MATCHES.

G. T. P. G. I'. P Leeds 1 1 5 Wakefield Trinity 1 1 5 Hunslet 5 6 28 Bramley Hull Hull Kingston It 2 1 7 York 1 0 2 Wigan 1 1 5 Widnes 0 1 3 Batley Keighley Pontefract Leigh 0 1 3 Oldham 0 0 0 St. Helens 1 1 3 Barrow Salford Halifax Swinton 1 2 8 R'dale Hornets 0 0 0

Warrington v. Hull. Only a very weak team represented Warrington at Hull on Saturday, most of the regular hack's being absentees. The home team played a determined game and recorded their first victory for two months. Teams: Hill—Goodfellow, back; Parry, Cook, W. H. Pullin, and Freear, threequarter backs ; G. Hall and Goddard, half backs ; Harrisork(captain), Major, Fulton, J. Lewis, Rhodes, Hargreaves, Kilburn, and llufton, forwards. Warrington—Knight, back; Widdeson, Jordon, Jones, and Harris, three-quarter backs ; Davies and Lloyd, half backs ; Boardman, Shugars, Heath, Thomas, Swift, 13ilton, Harmer and Preston, forwards. Referee, Mr. J. Bruckshaw, Stockport. Touch judges, Messrs. M. Carr,Hunslet,and S. Goldsmith, York. Almost throughout the whole of the first half Warrington acted on the defensive, but whatever else they lacked there was no mistaking the deadly tackling of the visitol•s, and it was only this factor which kept the Hullites from scoring time after time. At length. from a scrummage close to the line, Goddard dribbled over and scored, for Goodfellow to improve, and Hull led by five points at the interval. This was immediately reduced after the resumption, cross-kick by Jones giving Swift an easy try, which Preston failed to convert. Warrington were seldom dangerous again, and Hall had matters altogether in their Own favour. Two more tries were added by Kilburn and Parry, Goodfellow placing one goal, the result being: Hull, 2 goals 3 tries (13 points) ; War- rin gton, 1 try (3 points). .

BRITISH EMPIRE SOAP. —The new toilet soar of the the British Empire Soap Company, as to which an advertisement appears in another column—appears to be carryine all before it. It has already reached a proud .position in the world of soap, not only because of its Intrinsic merits, but on account of the widespread interest taken in the unique competition now going on. The company offers cash prizes to a large amount. The first is £lOO in cash and there are 10 prizes of £lO each, 20 of £5 each, 50 of £2 each, and 100 of £1 each. The competition has become so attractive that it is only a question of time for the British Empire Soap to establish the claim of the company that it "cleanses the world."

St. Peter's Church.

On Sunday afternoon an open service in connection with the Men's Bible Class was held at St. Peter's Church. There was a large congregation over which Mr. T. S. Steel presided. An address was deli rered by the Rev. D. R. James. Miss Lena Duckworth rendered "The Better Land" with effect, while Mr. Levi Richardson was successful in his two songs. Two solos were contributed by Mr. Leonard Auklaud in an effective manner. Messrs. Walker's Male Voice Party,conducted by Mr. Armstrong, sang two quartets in an excellent style and Mr. B. Wilkes played an organ solo entitled " Mount of Olive."

THE EXAMINER.

' I

===== 0002601_19060120_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
J&NUARY 2Jth 1906.

How often during the last WARRINGTON twelve months have our HAS Conservative friends been FOLLOWED! irritated by the motto, "Warrington to Follow" which has been persistently published by the Liberals whenever any notable triumph has been aclaieved at the bye-elections? Once they were provoked to reply with the old adage about count- ing chickens before they are hatched, but the chickens have hatched out nicely, and Warrington —good old Tory Warrington—has followed. But Warrington must no longer be called a Tory town; by one fell swoop its political colour title been changed as if through magic. The Tory blue is displaced by the Radical red, and for the first time in a quarter of a century Warrington returns a Liberal member to the House of Ominous. It is difficult to know how to approach Men- day's memorable victory. As Liberals we are unused to the sensations of tr:unsph, and the deportment proper under tke cireurnstmces is difficult to decide. The natural mm tempts us to exult, but exaltation over the fallen is not seemly. We will try, therefore, to discuss the Liberal triumph and the Tory downfall without our smiles becoming too apparent. Warrington Liberals are all smiles just now, and their jubilation would be very galling to the humbled Conservatives if the latter were only abroad to see it. By some mysterious process they seem to have vanished, and the scarcest thing in the War- rington streets since Monday has been the common or garden Tory. Like the rats and the foxes, as Mr. MADDEN suggested, he must have run to cover under the storm. But we are approaching n;gh the spirit of the gloater, which would ill-

become us. The antidote for this feeling of elation must surely be found in figures. Where are they? Here : CROSFIELD 5,599 PIERPOINT 4,099

1,500 There is a fullness, symmetry and roncdness about these figures which is almost poetic; in fact if you say them:quickly,thus :5599-4099-1500 and add "volleyed and thundered" they merge quite naturally into a well-known poem. We really must however subdue such little ecstacies and examine them more dispassionately. A week ago they would have been beyond the most frenzied anticipation. Then, the Conservative majority in Warrington stood at the alarmingly high figure of 1166, arid the Liberal poll had never risen to more than 3,326. It was unpleasantly obvious that there must be a trernendoss effort, impelled by a great revulsion of local feeling, to turn such a majority into even , the smallest minority, but the Liberals of War- rington were more than hopeful. We need not recapitulate all the circumstances which have,dur- iug the past two or three years of the Conservative reign,conspired to make the nation sigh for achange and Liberals grow increasingly coutident of an ultimate success at the polls in Warrington and all over the country. Suffice it to say that Mr. BALFOUR and his Government committed the crime, which is as unpardonable in politics as in society, of outstaying their welcome. It was only on Saturday, however, that the measure of their mischief was made apparent. That was the first day for polling in many constituencies, includicg Manchester. The result is too fresh in every mind to require elaborata treatment here. Manchester set an example unprecedented in political annals. There was, in fact the " clean sweep " which had only been spoken of in bated breath. The whole of the nine seats in the city and the neighbouring con- stituency ofSalford went Liberal—or Labour—with an overwhelming vote. The world and Warrington stood aghast,and so far as the latter was concerned the result of the contest between Mr.Crosfield and Mr. Pierpoint became a foregone concluiion. Still, there was room for hopes and fears on both sides locally. Warrington is a queer town, and its people take more than an ordinary amount of moving in a public sense. There were Tories wt.* thought that Mr. Pierpoint might stern the tide, but they were wrong. Warrington was fairly caught by the wave of public revulsion to Protec- tion and Chinese slavery, and the declaration of the poll gave the unprecedented majority of 1500 ,to °the Liberals. It was—and is—wonderful, magnificent! Last week we modestly counted on Mr. Crosfield's return, and pointed out that it only required 300 Conservatives to plump for the Free Trade ticket in order to assure that desidera- tum in our eyes. We were curiously correct in our figures. The poll shows that Mr. R. PIERFOINT'S vote has fallen trorn 4,468 in 1990 to 4,099, a decrease of 369. Probably the odd 69 did not vote, and the remaining 300 went solid for Mr. CROSFIELD. This, in itself, was jest sufficient to ensure a Liberal victory, taking the normal Conservative majority in Warringtou at 600. But the 1,500 majority transcended all probabilities. Mr. CROSFIELD'S figures rose from 3,303 in 1900 to 5,599, an increase of only four short of 3,000. There were over 900 more electors on the register than previously, and these have evidently been Liberal to a man, while the rest of the colossal majority comes from the fact that the gr.iss poll is also a record one. Out of 10,365 electors, no less than- O,7ll—counting 13 spoiled papers—went to the poll, so hunch stirred were the people of the town by the great issues involved. In common with almost every industrial centre in the land they sounded the death-knell of Protection and raised a solemn and effective protest against the employment of Chinese coolies in South Africa under conditions of slavery. The vote expresses more than this, when taken in conjunction with events elsewhere. It is obvious that the democracy is awakening to its powers and responsibilities. The Labour vote has received an enormous accession of strength all over the country, and the, days of Toryism, of the old-fashioned sort, would seem to be over. We are not, by any means indifferent to the fact that "those sweeping changes in popular feeling move in cycles, and that therefore the Tories may reasonably expect to see their turn come once again. The Labour movement is however, a factor which, they cannot overlook and which concerns them more nearly than it does the Liberals,because it threatens their very existence as a party. Labour may become merged eventually into Liberalism, or vice versa, but it can never have anything in common with Toryism. Such a thing as a Conservative Labour party is as much an impossible dream as it is a misnomer. Kirtu- nately, the Liberal party has been all along tolerant and friendly to the Labour cause, and with ideas largely in common they have pro- gressed on parallel limes until they are within measurable distance of. doing what parallel lines themselves never do—ce. coming together The future is unquestionably luminous with hope for the advancement of the Progressive forces, theo upclilfatsisne. uplifting of the masses and the disendownieut f the

It is a gratifying cir- THE VICTOR AND THE curostance that Warring- VANQUISHED. ton's new member is an honest broad-:ninded Liberal with quick sympathies for the masses, and he should be assured that solid backing from the democracy which has been, we believe, one of his political ideals. He will represent the people of Warrington as they have never been represented under the Conservative regime, and at the same time do much to restore the fortunes of the Liberal party in the town. The power of class interest in Warrington has been broken by Mr CROsFIELDR triumph and RS long as he remains the member for the Borough monopoly will have to take a back seat. We are sure that we are expressing the views of all Progressives when we wish him, in their name, the health and strength necessary to translate his fixity of purpose into reformative action as a member of the Council of the nation. As a closing word we should like, in this time of rejoicing for Liberals, to sound a note of sympathy with the man, who after enjoying tha confidence of the Warrington electors tor 14 years, should be so rudely dismissed as was the Case on Monday. Mr. R. Pierpoint has not been a commeudabie member from our point of view, but to him as a man we extend the hand of sympathy and good- will in the hour of his defeat.

The pulitical battle ori Monday was, we are convinced, the cleanest that has ever been fought in Warrington, and the credit to the Liberals for their magnificent victory is thereby rendered all the greater. It was a source of gratification to many people to bear the oft-repeated declaration at Liberal meetings that the Party was determined to carry on the Eight bY

?pure methods, and we believe that that promise was fulfilled. In days gone by, both parties iti Warrington have resorted to means of influencing the elector, which were morally iniquitous and illegal also, and we should not have been surprised to learn that our Tory friends were bone toa punctilious on Monday, but there is n.' evidence to hand that they trausgressed in any particular. Time was when the beer-barrel, played an imprtant part in Warrington elections .; and the Tories had almost a monopoly of the per- suasive fluid, but on IVlonday there was less .drunkenness and more honest voting than was ever known in the borough before. Whether the Tories have turned over a new leaf,as well as the Liberals, we do not know, hut little could be 'urged against them this time. The "Daily News" 'remarked on Tnursday with regard to election corruption, as follows : "We are afraid there is no doubt at all that some small constituencies have been held for Toryism by hribery,shainelessly direct in some cases, indirect in cone others, not less corrupting in purpose"and einnhasiz-A the need for a patition at Dover, where Mr. George Wyndham was re-elected in the Conservative interest. Allegations of a most serious nature •are made in respect of this constituency, and a petition has been decided upon. It speaks well for the advance Warrington has made in recent years on the lines of municipal purity that a keen `.Parliamentaly contest has been carried through without a single suggestion of nial-practice on 'either side.

To-day (Saturday) the THE FIGHT IN THE electors of the Knutsford KNIITSFORD Division have the oppor- tunity of wresting the seat

hem' Tory reptlesentation -and striking another telling blow at Protection. We believe that they will rise to the occasion after the style of Manchester and Warrington, for

1 i never has enthusiasm in the Progressive cause run . so high as at present. The Liberal candidate— Mr, A. J. King—is admirably qualified to represent the division. As a keen business man, and one who has also studied carefully the plsition of the agri- 'culturist, he is a convinced Free Trader, being fully persuaded that Protection would be disas- trous to the nation. His opponent—the Hon ' Alan de Tatton Eorton—has declared himself a " whole-hogger," but he has recently shown signs of regtetting his rashness in pronouncing for Mr. Chamberlain's measures.which are not at all popu- lar in the division. On the other hand, Mr King's staunch advoescy of Free Trade and many pressing reforms which benefit the farming element no less than town dwellers, has been .. received with enthusiasm. He bee displayed remarkable activity in pursuing the educational work in every part of the constituency which is about forty miles in extent, and deserves all praise for his courageous fight. Stockton Heath is fully realiz;ng its duty ii 4 this uiomentoas battle, and Mr. King may confidently rely upon a substantial backing from the constituents in that rapidly awakening neighbourhood.

The _electors in the Knuts- Is IT ford Division have this INTIMIDATION ? week received a communi- cation from the Hon. Alan de Tatton Egerton'a election agent which tran- scends anything in the way of cool cheek we have seen for a long time and which might very properly be looked upon in a more serious light. The offending document con- sists of a circular with an appended form for the elector to fill up, and was accompanied by a stamped envelope. It is nothing more or leas than a request to know how each man will vote. The reply form commences :—" It is my intention to record my vote at the above election in favour of tho Hon. Alan de Tatton Egerton ;" then follow a line for the voter's signature, and spaces for other particulars. We presume that the bulk of Mr. Egerton's tenants are voters in his division , how many of them dare do anything hut sign this form 9 The ciicular says, with a smug hypocrisy Which will arouse the ire of every fair-minded , voter : "In making this request there is no desire to infringe the secrecy ot the ballot." We assert emphatically that it does infringethe secrecy of the ballot, or attempt to do so, and in a most contemptible fashion. A more scandalous inter- ference with the elector's. privilege to vote as he likes without telling any man, we have never heard of, and we hope Mr. Egerton's constitnents will show their resentment of his un-English methods , by returning Mr. King with a crushing majority. ,

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Hats off, gentlemen, to A. 11. Crosfield, Esq., M.P. * * * Principles, and not personalities, won after all ! * * * A few real green grass sods would be very accept- able in front of the Town Hall. Elections have a blighting effect on the verdant carpet used by the multitude. * * Colonel Pilkington, the Conservative candidate for Newton division, has told the electors that he intends to follow Mr. Balfour. No doubt he will! *s* Carnarvon Boroughs poll to-day (Saturday) when Mr. li. A. Naylor who is stated to be quite conGdent of victory, will probably receive a shock ! * * * The"foreign labour" cry in Warrington apparently only served to remind the electors that the late Government introduced the worst kind of foreign labour into the South African Colony.. It certainly did Mr. Crosfield no harm ! * * We appeal to all Liberal owners of conveyances and motor-cars to give what assistance they can to the workers in the Kuutsford Division who are hoping to return Mr. King, to-day, Saturday. The committee- room in Stockton Heath is at l 6, London-road. * * * It was unfortunate that Mr. Pierpoint's torchlight procession on Saturday night reached Market Gate , just as the news of the -Manchester &Wade was becoming generally known. Few people felt inclined to cheer the Conservative candidate and the effort to -work up a demonstration was quite a failure. These Labour men have* won their way to St. Stephens by proved and strenuous service. They have known the weariness, the pain, and the naked- ness of the long struggle that has no playtime. They have been tried as by fire. They are nearer to the soul of humanity than we in our armchairs. Let them come, and let them speak. The Labour instinct is in •their blood. It will count for more than all our theories.—W. B. Hodgson, in the " Daily News." * The sweeping victories gained for Free Trade have resulted in some of the Tory papers letting -themselves go with a vengeance. Here is an ebullition of had temper culled from the " Manchester Courier," ' which may possibly have been specially hurt by the rejection of Mr. Sowler. "Large numbers of the Liberal candidates are Eighty Club fledglings, who have to thank an irre- sistible tide of influence " for their return to 1 Parliament. The House of Commons will certainly present a very unwonted appearance. It looks as though many constituencies would vote for a ,passable marionette if attired in Liberal colours .and adorned with a few appropriate phylacteries. We advise most of the inexperienced young gentle- men who are now with tmastonished delight adding M.P., to their names, to make the most of their time in the comin,, Parliament, for they will im- doubtedly never belong to another." * * * Sheffield seems to have been quite as much upset by the Manchester holocaustand the "Yorkshire Tele- 4avaph and Star " made the following hysterical appeal on the morning of the poll in Sheffield : "The fact that Manchester has gone clean out of Ito mind should be only a spur to Sheffield to redress the balance. The freaks of a political lunatic asylum can hover give the cue to a nation bent on serious work. The weak-minded inhabi- tants of Cottonopolis are no guides for the sturdy, burly, intelligent le orkshiremen. Sheffield has 'never followed ; it has always led, and to it now the country is looking with mingled anxiety and fear, from it millions of our fellow-countrymen ,across the sea expect a verdict more intelligent, more patriotic, MORE ENGLISH than that which has been rendered by the neighbouring county in an attack of political delirium tremens. A great respon3ibility rests upon us. If Sheffield goes right all will go right; upon our votes to-day may depend the fate of Empire.

EMI

What member of the Conservative Club was it who, when that notorious blue rocket went up, jumped on his hat for joy, kicked it in the fire, and " stood " champagne all round ? 5 5 5 "Is the candidate in favour of supporting a measure for the relief of his Majesty's unemployed ex-Ministers ? " was a question sent up to Mr. A. J. King at the Liberal meeting at Stockton Heath on Tneaday. Such a measure will be badly needed before long, but Mr. King did not promise his support. * * Warrington people were particularly delighted to hear of the return of Mr. Fred Maddison for Burnley and Mr. H. Vivian for Birkenhead. These gentlemen rendered yeoman services here by addressing several meetings on behalf of Mr. Crosheld and their great victories gave quite as much satisfaction in War- rington as in the constituencies where the campaigns were actually waged. _ * * _ _ _ _

There are some funny stories leaking out with re- gard to the premature rejoicings in Sankey street. We hear of one cute individual who had aprivate view of i the blue rocket and immediately went n for betting on what he thought was a " dead cert." Boldly he laid 100 talon Pierpoint getting in. A speculative friend took him in shillings. Five minutes later the actual result was received and the plunger had to pay out 15. * * *

The" Water Lily" at Winwick will remain on its beam.ends so far as compounding is concerned. In the face of the determined opposition by ratepayers and the final adverse vote in the Town Council the request to the Local Government Board for sanction to borrow ;f11,600 has been withdrawn. This is a dis- tinct saving to the town of about 5.3;200 and reflects credit upon the local economists. * * * _ . .

The Conservatives of the Knittiford Division at Stockton Heath are being appealed to in the following terms by their alarmed leaders : " Heathens keep sane " "Keep your tails up" and "Stem the flowing tide." We should imagine that it will take some very thick tails to stem the flowing tide. Mr. King is certain to get in we bear. He has made a point of appealing to the heads and not .the tails of his con stituents. * * *

Now that the elections have begun there is a still greater slump in "Mr. Chamberlain's proposals." Originally published at Is. net a reduction of 50 per cent, was recently made, but in London, at any rate, the public won't have them even M Id. A vendor was trying to get rid of them at this price in Fleet- street the other day but he sorrowfully told an inquiter : "I can't get anybody to buy em at a penny and I shall have to go in the banana trade ! " * * * Glasgow, like Manchester, has gone absolutely solid against Protection with an enormous turn-over of votes, At the last election, every one of the seven seats were held by the Conservatives. Now, only one Conservative survives the general rout, and he is a Free Trader. Mr. Bonar Law' the bright particular star of the younger Unionists has, like Mr. Balfour, received " the order of the knock." Mr. Pierpoint and he will doubtless have a fellow feeling for one ' another. They were on the Parr Hall platform together not very long ago. * * * _

Mr. A. J. King, the Liberal candidate for Kuutsford division, has been indefatigable in his efforts to place the issues of Saturday's fight before the electors in every part of the big constituency. It is not to be wondered at that while he was addressing a meeting at Knutsford on Wednesday, his voice failed him for a time. Mrs. King, however, stepped into the breach, and spoke vigorously against Protection. She effec- tively silenced an interrupter by the retort : • "I hope you will listen to a lady." No further interruption occurred. * * *

Warringtonians were more than ordinarily interested in the result of the Birmingham elections, by reason of the fact that Mr. rt. L. Outhwaite, the opponent of Mr. Chamberlain had addressed a huge meeting at Parr Hall in support of Mr. Crosfield. Liberals throughout the town were genuinely sorry to find that Mr. Chamberlain's majority over his plucky and able opponent was so large. " Joe's " personal popularity in the Midland capital must outweigh every other con- sideration if a man like Mr. Outhwaite can only poll 2,091 votes against him. Another matter for regret in these joyous times is the defeat by a narrow margin of Mr. F. Neilson in the Newport Division of Shrop- shire. Mr. Neilson is another candidate who has earned popularity with' Warrington electors, and it was hoped that be would overthrow Colonel Kenyon- Slaney, a typical representative of the landlord class. However he did the next best thing in reducing the majority from 1,285 to 160. Quite a moral victory ! * * *

Ten minutes before the declaration of the poll on Monday evening a blue rocket soared into the sky from the neighbourhood of the Town Hall. The assembled thousands roared out their applause, and at the Conservative Club " Pierpoint for ever " was toasted in generous style. Some of the more ardent and astonished Tories danced a pas Saul on the balcony, and all were deliriously happy. Then a red rocket cleft the heavens from the neighbourhood of the Con- servative Club itself, and the revellers were dumb- founded. A few momonts later, the actual result was shown on the Town Hall screen, and the terrific out- burst of cheering from the densely packed thousands drowned the moans of anguish in Sankey-street. The rockets were a hoax, and a somewhat cruel one, for it put both sides in terrible perturbation. Possibly the pyrotechnist got hold of the wrong colour first by mistake. Let us charitably hope so.

Warrington Bakers at Law. • On Monday in the Chancery Court of Lancashire at Manchester a motion ing the action James Horton Limited v. Horton waor. brought before the Vice- Chancellor, Mr. Octavius Leigh-Clare to restrain the defendant from committing a breach of an agreement. It was stated that Horton, who was formerly managing director of the plaintiff company, which carried on business at Warrington, as bakers, agreed on leaving the company not to carry on a similar business within ten miles. The defence was that the business carried on by Horton at Wilderspool within one mile of War- rington belonged to his irife. His Honour granted an injunction restraining the defendant from carrying on the business, or being interested therein. Plaintiffs were represented by Mr. Eastwood, (instructed by Mr. R. Evans' Warrington). Iron Fencing of every description. Lowest prioes.—J. S. r4_ARLAND, Si to 55, Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt. Grammar School Old Boys' Dinner. HUNG UP HIS HAT.

The Happy Man Who Married a Widow.

A jovial-looking little man, described as a green- grocer, sued at the Bloomsbury County Court, told Judge Bacon that ho had not paid because he was not a greengrocer now. Judge Bacon : Well, what are you now ? Defendant : Why, you see, your worship. Ido little odd lobs for a person in Monster-street. It's a lady, and I buys a few things for her in the market four morn- ings a week: Judge Bacon: Is the lady a greeogroc9r ? What is her name?

Defendant (confidentially) : It's Mrs. Swindells your worship. (Laughter.) inuge Bacon : Oh is it? (Laughter.) What rent do you pay? Defendant: I don't pay no rent. I live with my wife. The house is her property; it don't belong to me. You see I married a widow. Judge B icon: You hang up your hat in the hail; you have board and lodgings; s that all you get out of the greengrocery is clear profit. You are the happy man, for you say the widow keeps you. Order for £1 a month.

WESLEY GUILD ANNIVERSARY.

Vigorous Address by Rev. J. M. Gautrey.

THE CHRISTIAN DUTY.

In connection with the celebration of the anniversary of the Warrington Wesley Guild a well-attended rally WAS held in the Bold-street schools on Thursday even- ing the principal speaker at which was the Rev. J. Moffatt Gautrey, of Liverpool. Mr. Posnett of Runcorn bad been announced to preside but owing te a prior engagement he was prevented from doing so his place being taken by Mr. C. E. Parker. An excellent response was made to the roll call by the Rev. J. Manning, each Guild in thecircuit with the exception of Lyann being well represented. The Her. S. PITT said he was a convert to the Wesley Guild Movement. There was a great and pressing need for the many organizations which they as Wesleyans had associated with them. (Hear, bear). One promi- nent feature of the Guild was recreation and with that he was not disposed to quarrel so long as it was recrea- tion and not pastime or amusement. Although time might hang heavily on the hands of young people to merely pass away the time was unworthy and employ- ments which had nothing wider in view had no right to exist in Christian organizations. Recreation was something which ought to fit them to take their part in life and such a thing they should welcome and he was thankful that the Wesley Guild afforded an oppor- tunity for the teeming masses of young people to find recreation in the House of God. (Applaue.) There was element in connection with the Guild which was rather in the background and that was actual definite Christian service and until that was brought to the front and not until then would the Guild accomplish the work for which it existed. (Applause.) The Rev. J. M. GAUTREY said that when it was decided to form a Wesley Guild he did not think the proposal necessary in face of the existence of the Christian Endeavour Society, but since then he had discovered the wis.lom of the proposal. The Christian Endeavour might suit some churches but he was becoming more and more con- vinced of the fact that it would not suit the Wesleyans. The Wesley Guild if prop irlv worked was a tremendous stimulous to the Methodist Chnrch. The Guild did not exist for purposes of frivolity. It knew nothing of social dances or third rate theatricals, it existed to minister to the mental, moral, social, and spiritual uplifting of its members. Ha would take for the sub- ject of his address the text" How can I help England." In the2Oth century there was far too much let aloneism, too much of a tendency to impose upon posperity !the burden which should be borne to-day. If all triose who had mide the glorious traditions of our race had none upon those lines our lives would be sadly impoverished. (Applause.) We had to see that we handed down to our successors that heritage of freedom which was ours not unimpaired, but enriched by our having handled it. (Apnlauee.) We must cry out against the ancient sophistry that patriotism existed in the taking up of arms ti repel the invasion of our terri- tory. Patriotism was degenerating into Jingoism. (Heart hear.) Let them examine a 'crowd upon some great national festival. Was the cry raised an invocation ;or a psalm of praise ? No. The average British crowd shouted itself hoersevrith " Rule Britannia," Even our NationalAnthern was calculated to minister tat the insclar prejudices of our race. "Just fancy" said Mr. Gautrey "yhe imprudence of tea-thing a child in the schools to sing 'confound their palitics, frustrate their knavish tricks' as if every other nation in the world was a pick- pocket or vagabond. Patriotism and England have , been divorced and it is for us to see that they are re-united." Continuing he said the danger in England lay not outside but within the country. They could help'England more by fighting, in season and out of season, the demon of strong drink which ! WAS throttling the nation's life. (Applause). "There is no greater scandal" he said "in our Christianity than the inhuman drink traffic which is enslaving men body and soul. Here are we in the twentieth century holding little tinpot ' ! temperance meetings and talking pious platitudes to men and women who are convinced teetotallers and could not get drunk if they tried. It is time we had done with these and with the Miserable little committee meetings where we move resolit ions condeming the drink traffic. We are preaching to the wrong people. It is the raw material which we want, not only at our , temperance meetings but in our churches. We have to wake up to this question and have done with the matter- of-fact and more respectable form of tempemnce propa- ganala. We have to see to it and by any means which we can destroy the evil which is destroying so ! many fellow countrymen." (Applause.) Another way in which we could help England, continued Mr.Gantrey is by working against the prevailing practice of desecrating the Sabbath. It was this desecration and indifference which was going to the root of the whole of our national life and it must be resisted. It was only by following Christ that we could be true to the destinies of England and could pass down enriched our noble heritage of freedom. (Applause.) The proceedings closed with the pronouncing of the Benediction. The celebration of the Anniversary commenced on Sunday when two well attended services were held in Bold street Church. 'Thursday's proceedings opened with a service at which the Rev. J. M. Gautrev was the 'preacher, followed by a tea to which an excellent num- ber sat down.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee will be held at the Town Hall at six o'clock on Monby evening when the bnsiness for consideration will include the following :

PhYSICAL INSTRUCTION. At a meeting of the School Management Committee it was resolved that, provided a sufficient number of the teachers now attending the classes in physical exercises held at the Gymnasium desire to present themselves for such examination, arrangements be inade with the Incorporated British College of Physical Education for an examination of these teachers to be conducted by that college at the close of the present course of instruction. TITO SMONDARY SCIIOOL. The committee resolved that the resignation of Miss Lily 11. Warburton, assistant mistress at the Secondary school and Technical Institute, be accepted; her engagement to terminate on the 31st January, 190 G. The action of the director in issuing an advertisement inviting applications to fill the vacancy thus caused was approved, and it was resolved that. a sub-committee, consisting of Mr. A. J. Jolley, Mr. J. Upson, the Rev. Neville Wilson, and the Chairman of the Committee (the Rev. Canon Stevenson), be appointed to consider the applications received and to select candidates to appear before the committee at their next meeting. Reports were submitted from the Principal of the I Secondary school upon the work of the (a) pupil teachers ; (b) preparatory pupil teachers : (c) scholars holding free studentships ; and (d) students who have been admitted free of charge, during the past term. The Director was requested to draw the attention of the parents in some cases to the unsatisfactory nature of the reports, and so express the hope that steps will be taken to secure improvement in the future.

THE PROTECTIONIST ROUT.

Free Trade sweeping through the country.

===== 0002601_19060120_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
-- GREAT LIBERAL REVIVAL.

More ex.ministers defeated.

Over-throw of LORD STANLEY and Mi. BRODRION. •

IThe elections which have proceeded throughout the 'week have indicated in an unmistakable way that the ; great English working-class boroughs will have nothing to do with Mr. Chamberlain's Protectionist heresy, or Mr. Balfour's nebulous scheme of Retalia- tion. Free Trade Nis been the watchword, and right staunchly has the democracy stood by the policy which lhas been the foundation of England's commercial ! greatness and industrial prosperity. Commencing with Ipswich and. Manchester, the forces of Protection and re-actionary Conservatism were utterly routed in almost every constituency, the Liberal and Labour gains being remarkable in : their number and completeness. Only Birmingham withstood the onslaught of the flowing tide, and here IMr. Chamberlain's personality was sufficient to retain his own and the other seats in the City on behalf of ; Protection and Conservatism by substantial majorities.l 1 The exception from the rule, at Birmingham, only serves to mike the victory of the Free Trade and Pro- gressive forces all the more remarkable, London —with the exception of the City—has swung round completely and declared for Free Trade. Out• of 59 boroughs in the metropolitan area the Liberall laud Labour parties have captured 32 from the Con- servatives,and now hold 40 of the 59. The Liberal vote , has increasad by 290,4111. Two of the greatest Free ' Trade victories, since Manchester, have been in New- castle and Sunderland. In the first-named place, two Conservatives were routed by majorities approaching 7,000, and in the latter by majorities of about 5,000; I Lord Stanley, whose elegant epithets of " blood- suckers and blackmailers " applied to the Postal employes will long be remeMberecl, was defeated by I 3,198 voles by Mr. W.T. Wilson (Labour) in the West- houghton Division. Thus vanished yet another mem- ber of the late Government.

Glasgow has also gone solid for Free Trade. Thare are Liloeral gains in four divisious,a Labonr victory in a fifth, while the other two divisions are held by Con- servative Free Traders. _ _

The election results declared yesterday g,ive the first indication of the trend of opinion in the English counties. We are glad to say that it proved to be as strong in the direction of Liberalism; as feeling in the boroughs has been. In 27 constituencies the Liberals gained thirteen seats and lost only one—St. Albans Division of Herts. _

_ . But even more remarkable was the defeat of ea-Ministers. Mr. Brodrick, formerly War and then Indian Secretary, Mr. Ailwyn FeHowes and Captain Pretyman, members of Mr. Balfour's Government, were all defeated. Rejected also • was Mr. Chaplin, the most consistent Tory Protectionist in the, country and the mover of the Protection Resolution at Newcastle that led to hr. Cham- berlain's speech and Mr. Balfour's collapse. On the polls declared at night Mr. Scott Dickson, Lord Advocate in the late Government, 'end Mr. Bonar Law, who was Parliamentary Secretarftto the Board of Trade, AI ere both defeated at Glasgow. One seat was captured by hr. Ferens (L) at east Hull and another by Mr. Nuttall at Stretford, Lancashire.

MAJORITY ON A DIVISION.

Each Liberal gain counts two votes on a divi- sion, since a gain of a seat means that there is one fewer Conservative member and one more Liberal. At the dissolution the Conservative majority on a division in the House was 67. It has now been wiped out, and "we have instead a Liberal majority (including th,3 Labour and Nationalist members returned) of 205.

The Liberal Future.

Sir,—After the signal achievement of Monday last the 'Progressive forces should gtw from strength to strength. The great and glorious 'Free Trade" issue might not have to be defended again for some years to come and so Liberalism will to some extent depend on its splendid principles. Of course future efforts will he making the retention of the seat certain and with common action, cemented by this knowledge, it is hoped a solid substantial structure will be raised that shall I abide for years by Monday's example. W. F. i Latchford.

Election Crowds in Warrington. Sir,—l cannot let the Parliamentary Election of 190 G go by without paying a tribute of admiration to the splendid behaviour,good humour and sound common- sense of the Warrington populace, as seen in the huge crowds at the various meetings and other dernonstra- tidos during the past fortnight. I have done duty at elections in almost every large city in England, and have never seen crowds so well conducted, tractable and fair minded as in 'Warrington. They are sometimes lively and enthusiastic, often earnest and impos- ing, but never brutal or violent. This I have had the pleasure of saying before in the columns of your paper on other occasions of ' excitement, such as the Declaration of Peace, Corona- tion, Mafeking rejoicings, &c., but during the past fortnight, considering the intensity of public feeling, Warrington excelled itself in the right direction. For this happy state of affairs I can only thank my fellow townsmen one and all ; they have covered themselves with credit, and have set a pattern which other towns might do well to emulate, in the matter of good humoured conflict; and, incidentally, they have made for me and my men a pleasure of what might have been

OawglMlNNOVaimih

an arduous and anxious duty. T am proud to be the E. Hobson, W. Lloyd, W. &mister, W. Cheetham, T. servant of such a Public, Thanking you in anticipation, Colerup, and E. Yates. I am, Dear Sir, Faithfully Yours, LUKE TALBOT, Chief ConstAble. Luckily, the match was only a friendly. Chadderton were the winners,and they played capital football right through, the Warrington backs showing nothing like the com- bination which was expected of them. They have some excuse in the fact that Short sustained another injury to the shoulder and had to retire from the game before half-time. Knutsford Division: Tory Tactics !

Sir,—As the day of election draws near the Tory candidate is painfully exhibiting his terror of the fate which has already befallen his Protectionist collea cues all around him by the blind manner of his lighting at the very time that coolness and truth are most essential.

lAfter running round the constituency for two or three weeks bearing aloft his straw-stuffed Guy Fawkes " Home Rule", he seems to have discovered that—in spite of the smoke-clouds which are requested at his meetings—the working man has seen the stuffing coin- ing out and has discovered the double-died Protection- ist candidate behind it, vainly endeavouring to make the stuffed figure look alive and brandish his shilialegh. So blindly is he now fighting that he has altogether forgotten the grand old Tory principles under which he started this campaign with such a flourish of trumpets. Tariff reform, Colonial preference and Imperialism and all the rest of the true blue " programme are flung away, and he seems to have taken madly to the cry, as well as the colours, of his Radical opponents. Were it a case of " sudden conversion " it would indeed be a matter for Liberal rejoicing, butas that is very doubtful the strange picture of a " whole-hogger " crying " No tax on food and" The big loaf" in his opponents' colours is indeed anything but a consistent one. These panic-stricken tactics will—l am sure—he seen through quite as clearly as the other, and -there is 130 W. little ckubt but that the electors in this portion of the Knuts- ford division will join hands with Manchester, Oldham, Preston, and last but not least—Warrington—in laying low the selfish demon of "Protection by Taxation " for many a year to come. King for Kuutsford division on Saturday !—Yours faithfully,

W. H. SHERBURN.

===== 0002601_19060120_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
Newton Division Election.

Sir,—Having had some little share in three previous Parliamentary contests, in the Newton Division, I naturally take some interest in its present prospects, and am anxiously waiting to see what will be the issue of the struggle now going on in the Division. One cannot but feel that it is a desperate struggle for class ' ascendancy as against the well-bein,, of the general community. There have been many fights in the past, but none that equal the present in importance or in its far-reaching consequences. Each side in this battle is worthily represented. For class ascendancy no better champion could be found than Colonel Pilkington. For the common good—the well-being of the many as against the few—stands brave and worthy Jim Seddon.' The question for each voter must be, "Which will best, represent my interests and principles?" To this ques- tion every honest voter will endeavour to give an honest reply and moreover will record his reply in the ballot box on the 23rd inst. The constituency is largely composed of the sons of toil, and to these, the main issue brought to the front in this election must appeal with peculiar emphasis. It is not necessary now to argue the pros and cons for Free Trade ; these are, by this time, as well understood as they are likely to be. But the one object in view should be for every voter to exercise the high privilege he possesses of giving effect to the opinions he holds with regard to the issu) now before the country. Of course the question of Free Trade is not the only one which conceins the intelligent working man, though, on this occasiou,it is the one that comes first in order for settlement. There may be many points of difference, in details, between the individual voter and the candidates now seeking his suffrage, but, if every working man, and every ether man, who is engaged in any commercial or industrial pursuit, will examine his own interests and especially those, of the country at large, with an open mind, there will not be much doubt as to which of the gentlenr-n now before the constituency will be its elected representative on Tuesday next. Let every working man be true to his manhood, and to his great responsibility and, if after serious thought ihe considers that he will be best represented by Colonel Pilkington, let Colonel Pilkingtou have his vote. If, on the other hand, Jim Seddon•is the roan most likely to serve, not only a class but the entire community, let him see to it that his conscience is clear from the guilt of a neglected duty in the face of a great and serious crisis in the nation's history. If there is one thing morp clear than another at this moment it is the serious earnestsess which is now manifesting itself throughout the land. Let the Newton Division electors imbibe that spirit of earnestness and realize the responsibility DOW resting upon them and by this means share in the glorious triumphs which are changing the very life of country. AN OLD FIGHTER IN THE CAUSE. Sale, January ISth, 1906.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

[By " Ovalist."]

I aim told that the Warrington men shaped like schoolboys at Hull last Siturday. They had a team no better than themselves to face, hut they did not go into the fray with anything like the enthusiasm apparent in the match with Leeds at Warrington.Away from home, Warrington have shown very little reso- lution this season, and the conviction is borne upon one that the !nen, as a whole are not triers. From the start they never made any attempt t) secure the , championship,and have all along been content to main- tain a moderately respectable positior by picking up : points here and there, principally at home. The abandonment of a work clause has not been any advan- tage to either club or team. In fact the laziness which has resulted is lowering the standard of play all round. Northern Union foothill has been at a distinctly low ebb this season.

Individually, only. about three of the Warrington men deserve any praise for their play at Hull. Lloyd was the best of the backs with Harris and Widdeson next, while the forwards were only moderate, Knight again played.below form, and the centres were weak Hull gave, a much better display and pleased their supporters with a very welcome win. Pullin, a new Welsh centre made a good impression.

, . The best perbrinance of the day was put up by.l Leigh, who went to Oldham and beat the leaders. Last season, Oldham were morally beaten at home by both Leigh and Warrington, but their unbounded luck saved them, This time, Leigh at any rate have gsit their deserts. It was a bitter pill for the Watershedd- ings crowd, as it deposed the home club from the leadership. Hunslet went to the top, and on Tuesday they strengthened their position with a win at Batley . Having several matches in hand they look like keeping Oldham in subjection.
