THE GREATEST MEETING OF THE CAMPAIGN.

SIR J. T. BRUNNER ON THE EVILS OF PROTECTION.

A Challenge to Local Protectionists

The closing meeting of the Liberal campaign which was held in the Parr [fall on Saturday evening,will ever rank as a memorable one in the history of Warrington. Long before the time announced for the commence- ment, the hall was crowded in every part and hundreds sought admission in vain. An overflow mooting was held in Cairo-street schools and at this the attendance was extremely large. 'l.'h• enthusiasm which prevailed thoughout the proceedings showed that there was a determination to win a great victory for Free Trade, and Mr. Crosfleld had a magniticant reception. Sir John T. Tit-under. But.. presided and was supported by Mr. A. FLCrosfield,M rs. John Crosfield,and Miss Crosfield. Mr. and Mrs. C. E. Parker, and many prominent Liberal workers.

The CFTAIRM tx delivered a powerful address On the question of the day. He said he was present in the capacity of capitalist standing in favour of a brother capitalist, and he advised the democrats of War- rineton when they had a capitalist tame to be sure to keep him. Mr. Crostleld and he were men wile ought to please everybody. They were Liberal candidates : they were Labour candidates—(cheers)— they were Unionist candidates, because they did not wish to destroy the union of the United Kingdom— ! (cheers)—and they were Tariff Reform candidates, because they wanted to reform the tariff by taking off the tax on sugar to begin with. (Cheers.) If the country returned Protectionists to Parliament he and their candidate would get rich beyond the dreams of avarice, for Protection would benefit the manufacturers at the expense of all who were not manufacturers. The temptation put by Mr. Chamberlain before the wealthy men of ,the country was a temptation that was almost irresistible, and no wonder that some of them had succumbed. He was told that nearly every manufac- turer in Warrington was a Proteetionist.—(" Not all.") ! In the United States when a tariff was under discussion, the head of one trade or another was asked what he would like the import duty to be, and in many c tses one man alone had been allowed to fix the duty upon the article that he in particular made. There was one concern in this country making 400,000 tons of steel rails a year. An import duty of lOs. a ton on steel rails would produce on such an amount £200,030. " Does that ring in your ears," cried Sir John, "as it does in mine, as a token of an awful temptation ? Imagine the Board of that Company discussing the chance of obtair- int', an import duty enabling them to raise the price of 400,000 tons a year. One says to the others : If we were to spend in buying votes £200,000 a yeer we should invest our money at the rate of 100 per cent per annum' —which is a pretty good dividend.—(Laughter.) If £lOO,OOO were divided among members of Parliament who may have been pretty heavily hit at the Derby— (laughter),—and it is pretty well known in the House of Commons, believe me. who are the needy men and who are not,—if £lOO,OOO were spent in buying the necessary votes that would mean an investment at 200 per cent, an annum." In 1886,when one of those mischievous, crazy motions in favour of Fair Trade was being discussed in the House of Commons, he instanced how the alkali industry would be affected if a duty wereimposed upon ! foreign sulphur ores in favour of Irish ore which was ' considerably dearer. Every Irish member, he said. would vote for that duty and a Protectionist Parliament would be likely to find among the members for Great Britain a majority in favour of a Protective duty. its it was amongst voters before they had the ballot there would always be on those (occasions, some wno were in doubt ns to which way their vote ought to go until they saw which side had the better money. There would be hesitating votes in the House of Commons—because under a Protective system men got elected in order to sell themselves. He would be there in this position. He was the only manufacturer in the alkali trade in England who wanted no sulphur. If he could compel his competitors to pay five (times more than now for their sulphur they would have to praise their prices and he could raise his. "And," continued Sir John, "I said in the House, to make it as brutal as I could, I can afford to spend £lOO,OOO to buy the necessary votes.' The Tories howled at me in derision. but I said, and I should make half a million profit a year out of it.' I said, I pray that this House may never put its hand to that foul and filthy " (Cheers). Continuing Sir John saki they were told over and over again that Protection would raise wages here. He thought it was extremely unlikely in that Protection had never raised the wages elsewhere. He said without fear of contradiction that there was no old country—he did not speak of the new coun- tries where men were scarce—where wages were so high, where hours were so short, and where the men's wages went further in procuriug for them the necessaries and comforts of life as in this dear old country of ours. (Cheers.) Protection would be an immensely good thing for him ; but as for the working men they would never get any more wages. He never knew manufac-

Referring to Mr. Balfour's speech the other day, in which he said that the Liberals were the spoliators of the licence holders, Sir John said that if he had had his way when the Licensing Bill was passing through the House of Commons he would have secured for the licence holder a certain minimum amount out of the compensation fund. IN NEWTON DIVISION.

MR. J. A. SEDDON'S CANDIDATURE.

Enthusiastic Meeting at :Sankey.

A determined effort is being made in the Newton Division to wrest the representation of the constitu- ency from the hands of the Conservatives. Colonel Pilkington, the late member, has a strong opponent in the person of Mr. J. A. Seddon, who is fighting in the interests of labour, and whose supporters are confident that he will be at the hesd of the poll on Tuesday next. A meeting in support of the labour candidate was held in the Sankey schools on Wednesday evening, under the chairmanship of Mr. C. E. Parker. There was a crowded attendance and considerabls enthusiasm was manifested.

Mr. Seddon, who had a hearty reception, delivered a brief address. They wore, he said, at tat time in the throes of one of the greatest struggles, in a political sense, that they had ever witnessel. They were presented with one issue of great magnitude the decid- ing of which one way or the other world make for the progress of England or would mean the uprooting of our glorious traditions and the making of our name a bye word amongst the nations, (Hear, hear.) As a citizen and a trade unionist he had a heavy indictment against the late Gove:nment for their extravagance which had been carried to the height of polling, (Cheers.) The manner in which the increased cost had been met was such as justified the strongest resentment of every workingman. Things which were part and parcel of a workingman's table had been taxed but when a resolution was moved in the House of Commons that fresh sources of revenue should be opened out by , a graduated system of &come tax, and the taxation of , land values and urban sites, it was opposA by the rich men who in effect said" tax the workingman us much as you like but don't put on a tax which will fall heavily upon our shoulders. (Cheers.) They thought they could tax the democracy because the democracy had been a patient animal in days past and had repeat- , edly sent monied men to Parliament to represent them ' with the result that their interests had been neglected and the interests of the rich looked after. (Cheers.) As a Trade Unionist he also opposed the late Govern- ment. Until recently Trade Unions had been protected by laws which were a monument, to the heroic sacrifice of the men who had gone before them. (Hear, hear.) One morning they woke up and found that from judge-made law the privileges which they enjoyed had been taken away. (Elear,hear). If they permitted that,if they permitted a judge to read into a law something which it was never intended should be read into it they were doing something which woald endanger the constitution of the country.(Cheers) Speaking gm the question of Chinese labour Mr. Seddon said that when he thought of promises made before the war and the events which had transpired since he asked himself" Is it passible thit a mall with as spark of manlinessab him, with one drop of British blood in his veins, can support the betrayal of the British race by the introduction of Chinese into South Africa." (Cheers). From every point of view, whether economic, industrial or moral the introduction of Chinese was an insult to the people of England and an outrage upon 1 their liberties and privileges. (Cheers.) California and Australia had tried Chinese labour and now would not have it at any price, and yet we had permitted international aliens in their thirst for gold to plant in South Africa a moral hell which jt, would take genera- tions to thoroughly stamp out. (Cheers.) Mr. Seddon in conclusion spoke strongly in support of Free Trade. There were in England, he said, 12,000,000 people who were constantly on the verge of starvation. A tax on their food, however small, would intensify their sufferings and send them below the poverty line. (C heels.) The Rev. J. Howard asked the candidate's views on the Education Act and what he intended doing if returned?

Mr. Seddon : I believe in trusting the people. I believe in religious instruction.As a democrat I say that the parents themselves, if they are in a sufficient majority ought to decide what teaching the children are to have. The Rev. J. Howard: Wrist are your views on Home Rule?

Mr. Seddon : Seventy years ago the land in Ireland was valued at £10,000,000 but for 70 years we have per- mitted rack-rentitr, landlords to pillage that country; we have permitted Ireland's best sons and daughters to cross the seas and become enemies of this country. So far as I am concerned I fear the Irish landlord more than the Irishman, and am prepared to trust the Irish to manage their own affairs. (Cheers.) Mr. Seddon, in answer to Mr. Dodds, said he did not think the question of Home Rule would come before the next Parliament.

Mr. C. E. Parker said he was pleased to support Mr. Seddon because he had for a long time been of the opinion that the different sections of the community should be represented in Parliament according to their numbers. There was no doubt that in the past the capitalist had been over-represented in the British House , of Commons—(hear, hear)—and that it was neces- sary that there should be an increased direct representation of workers in the House of Commons. (Cheers), Colonel Pilkington, speaking in that room the previous night, had said that .CL40,000,000 worth of manufactured articles were imported into England last year but he forgot to mention that of that amount all but.£30,000,000 worth were partly manufactured and were required by English manufacturers to whom it was essential that they sh auld be as cheap as possible. (Cheers). Mr. Parker proceeded to contrast the condi- tions existing in Germany with those under which the British worker lived. In the tanning trade he said wages were 45 per cent, lower than they were in England, the hours of work were longer and the wages would not exchange for the same amount of goods. (Cheers). (A voice " What about the Canadian preference ?"). It was a singular fact that the while exports from England to Canada had increased since the preference was given,the increase in exports from the United States to the Dominion was about double that from. England.

- Mr. T. Halton rose to speak and was called to order by the Chairman. Mr. T. Halton : Am I not to be allowed to speak ? (Cries of" No," "Chuck him out," and uproar). Mr. Parker : When the other gentlemen who support Mr. Seddon have spoken I am quite willing to listen to anyone and an prepared to stay here all night to dis- cuss the question. (('.heers.) Proceeding, Mr. Parker said that it was not fairor right that theranadian pre- ference was being used in support of Mr.Chamberlsin's propaganda. Canada had never asked us to tax the food of the people in return for that preference. Mr. Pownall : What about the manufactured goods which come into England without paying any duty ? Mr. Parker: That is something like the parson one hears about Mr. Pownall : We don't want to hear about the narson. Answer my question and " blow " the parson. I am a seller of American goods and —(the remainder of Mr. Pownall's remarks were inaudible owing to the uproar which arose.) Mr. Prust rose Vs propose a resolution in support or the candidature of Mr. Seddon. Mr. Pownall and Mr. Ha Iton rose simultaneously, and for a time all three gentlemen attempted to address the meeting at the same time. Mr. Parker made a vain attempt to restore order, pandemonium reigning for some five minutes. Teen Mr. Pownall resumed his seat, leaving the field to Mr. Halton. This gentleman attempted to address but was howled down. As he sat down Mr. Pownall again rose amidst the " boos " and " hoots " of those present, only to sit down immediately, as Mr. tialton again jumped to his feet, and proceeded to make his way in the direction of the platform.

Mr. Pownall again rose but a threat to forcibly remove him pro- duced quietness and Mr. Prust resumed his speech. The resolution was seconded by Councillor Rudd of St. Helens who in an able speech showed that the loss of the glass trade was not due to our fiscal arrange- ments but to the fact that our manuracturers were behind the times. He particularly instanced the flint glass trade, in which he personally was directly interested, pointing out that in Germany, by improved methods the manufacturers produced 108 tons with the same amount of fuel as was required in Eagland to pro- duce lb tons. From that, he said, it would be judged that the German manufacturer was in clover but such was not the Case. The highest profit made by a German glass firm was 7 per cent.and several manufacturers had to depend for a profit upon the bounties paid by the Government—bounties derived by taxing the workers. Mr. Rudd pointed out that in Germany the glass worker worked from 70 to B'J hours a week and received in wages 123. Gd. The speech was freely punctuated by interruptions from Mr. Pownall, but ultimately that gentleman left the _room.

Mr. Redford supported the resolution and in doing so appealed for workers on behalf of Mr. Seddon._

Colonel Pliklngton at Padgate.

Lively Meeting.

On Wednesday evening Colonel Pilkington and his supporters held a meeting in the Padgate schools,the re being a numerous :attendance of electors. The meeting was called for 8 o'clock but when the time arrived, although there WAS a large audience there were no signs of the Chairman or Colonel Pilkington. As the minutes passed, so ne of the local Liberals beseeched some of the prominent Conservatives by all means to commence a meeting—but the events of the week evidently had knocked all the spirit out of them,for they failed to rouse themselves to action. We prose ne that the shadow of forthcoming defeat—or fear of it—was disturbing them, for they wore a wearied and worried look. However shortly after 8.30 the Colonel pleased his supporters by putting in an Anne:v-411cm, but it appeared that he had left his Chairman, Alderman Francomb behind, addressing the electors of Woolston and district. Mr. Mather thereupon took the chair and at once introduced the Candidate. Colonel Pilkington in an able and lucid speech expounded his views and he , was on the whole patiently listened to, though there ' was at one time a lively passage of arras between him and Mr. George Walker over Mr. Herbert Gladstone's views about Chinese Labour. Mr. Pilkiugton read an extract from a newspaper and Mr. George Walker told Mr. Pilkington direct that he must know that Mr. Herbert Gladstone had contradicted the report. Mr. Pilkington startled his audience by saying that when Chinese I abour was first mentioned he thought of voting against it, but after explanation he changed his ' mind and voted for it. lie also strongly supported the late Government's policy and considered that the Education Act and the Licensing Act were steps in the right direction. Mr. Alderman Bennett supported fitr. Pilkington and as usual spoke most enthusiastically about Mr.l Chamberlain and the Empire. He said that the I, country appeared to him to be going mid and 17.1.3 not at all in a rational frame of mind,as the people were be- ing misled by false representation. The Alderman spoke so strongly that he fairly roused the opposition, crie3 ' of "Draw it mild Arthur" being heard. After other speeches in support, the Colonel was asked many questions, and he a iroitly answered them by talking "round about," au excellent way of avoiding giving a decided answer. The resolution in support of Colonel Pilkington's candidature was then put, the majority voting in its favour. This so delighted some of the local C.mserva- tives that they jumped on shair feet awl gave three cheers for the Colonel. But their opponents were not dismayed, and on a shout for three cheers for Seddon betng raised, it was responded to in no unmistakable manner, showing that there was at large opposition present. The meeting was, shortly after to.no, brought to a close by the usual vote of thanks to the Chairman.

P.S.A. Society Anniversary.

The thirteenth anniversary of the Warrington P.S.A. Society was celeberated on Sunday in the Co-operative 11111, Sankey-street, when an open meet- ing was held at which there was an excellent attend- ance of members :ind. friends. Mr. R. Nelson (vim.- president) took the chair, and in atmliort address spoke of the progress of the social and spiritual work of the society. The President (Mr. E. Wynne Jones) gave an address on "The motto for the year." Two solos " Light in darkness " and " Sun of my soul " were rendered in a very able manner by Miss Woodward, •of Stockton Heath ; Ur. 13. Wadsworth read the lesson ; and Mr. W. Brown also took part in the meet- ing. Twenty-four new members were welcomed into the society.

SOCIAL WORK AT MESSRS. GROSFIELD'S.

PRESENTATIONS TO OLD EMPLIYEES.

Speech by Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P.

THE ANCI

ODDS AND ENDS.

MOST paths are thorny, but wise people Keep their eyes open, and avoid stepping on the prickles. ONLY a boy with a wooden horse and an imagination knows what genuine rapid transit is like. _

AGREED WITH Hni.—" Father says you couldn't support me in style." 'No; he would have to make allowance for that."

IF some people would hunt for work with the conspicuous energy they use when hunting for trouble, one could make something of them. SOMETIMES a man is willing to remain at the foot cf the ladder for the purpose of pulling others down. "WHAT comfort do you. get out of being a fatalist?" "Don't you think it is a comfort to know that you couldn't help making an ass of yourself." BETTER. STTLL.—"Ma, kin I go over and play wid Micky Hoolihan?" My Dear Children,— Next week I hope to give the result of the competition for the making of small articles of clothing for the poor, I am glad to say that I have received a nice number of things. 'I his gives me great pleasure as it shows me that you have thought of the poor children this cold weather. I am very glad that you all liked the wine essence sent you by Messrs. Newball & Mason, I have to thank William Turney and others for the solution of puzzle that was sent with the wine essence. Many of you have written me very nice letters, these I cannot possibly answer personally, as some of you seem to think. If you write me nice interesting letters I will publish them, or better still, send me short stones and pieces of poetry, these need not be original, and as I have said before, I will award prizes to the best con- tributions sent me. John Longbottom sent me a little letter and a very pretty Christmas Card for which I thank him very much. Next week I hope to print a contribution from Alice Latham, this week I have not sufficient space. I have a great many new members, and I shall print a few names each week. You will all be pleased to hear that we have over 700 members for the Children's Corner League. I must not write any more this week as my Space is getting short, so will say good-bye.

With all good wishes, From UNCLE NED.

===== 0002601_19060120_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
DEAR UNCLE NED,-

I am writing to thank you very much for the nice box of chocolates you sent me, I was much surprised and very pleased when I received it. We began our Christmas Holidays on Friday, December 22nd, and we return on January 9th. I am enjoying Christmas very much, and I hope you are doing the same. I had a great many Christmas Cards sent me this year, and Lily and I are sending you one of our cards, wishing you a Merry Christmas and a Bright New Year. Well, Uncle Ned, it is the first prize I have received from you, and I hope it will not be the last, for I shall always think of you when I eat any of the chocolates, and I shall see how long I can keep the box in memory of you. Dear Uncle, you must forgive me this time for being so long in thanking you for that lovely prize. Well, I must bring my letter to a close. Thanking you very much for the chocolates.

I remain, your loving niece, ALICE COATES.

[P.S.—I am sending you a pair of baby's socks, and I hope you will be very pleased with them. I thought they would be very suitable to give away to the poor. I hope I shall be one of those who will succeed in your competition, in receiv- ing one of your prizes.]

-:0 : - A HEROIC BROTHER. A CONTRIBUTION.

Robert and his brother: James were one day passing a church where some men were at work. They had gone for their dinner, so the two boys climbed a ladder which enabled them to reach the roof. Soon they made their way to the end of the church which was the tower or belfry. There was a little opening in the tower, and through this the boys could see the country for miles around. "We must go down," suddenly exclaimed Robert, "there are the men returning to their work, they will be angry if they find thaT we have climbed the ladder.' The two boys hurried out of the belfry and along the rafters, until they reached the roof again. In their haste they both stepped on the end of a small plank and their weight made the plank turn over. Both boys lost their balance. Robert had just time to clutch hold of one of the beams as he fell, and he clung on to it with all his might. James missed the beam, but managed to sicze his brother round the legs, and he held on in despair. In this awful position the lads hung, shouting for help. But the workmen were still too far off to hear their cries, and as it was not yet time for them to commence work, they were not hurrying At last the strain of the double weight became

AN ADVENTURE WITH A MAN-EATER

It was during the hot weather of 1876 that, in company with a friend who was an officer in one of the native Indian regiments, I went on a shooting expedition for a few days in Travancore, Southern India. We were some days' march from any English settlement, and were on our way to pass the night at a native village, said by our guide to be near at hand. We had with us two sowars, or troopers, of my friend's regiment, who acted as shikarees, or hunters, to beat up the game and make themselves generally useful in camp. We were not looking especially for tigers, but were ready for anything that came; and we soon arrived at the village where we were to pass the night. What a lovely place it was, and how cool and pleasant it seemed to our tired eyes and over- heated bodies! It was built on the shore of a small lake, or " tank," and was shaded by groves of palm and cocoanut trees, and altogether there was an aspect of peace about it that was very pleasing. But when we came near, we were con- siderably astonished to hear none of the usual signs of welcome Usually when a Europeanenters a native village he is saluted by the furious barking of innumerable curs, and the inhabitant eagerly flock to see the sahib. But now all this was want- ing, and everything was as silent as the grave. Not a sign of the inhabitants was to be seen, and, as we went from door to door seeking some one and failed to find a living soul, we thought we had found a city of the dead. We were about to give up our quest, when from one of the huts their crawled a man, bent with age. Slowly he approached with many salaams, and in reply to our queries as to what had become of the rest of the inhabitants, informed us that they had all forsaken the town on account of a man-eating tiger. He was the only person left, being too old to leave Ha home. He informed us that the terrible tiger had visited the village three times, and each time had borne away a victim. Then the people could endure the danger no longer, and all had fled. "But, oh !" continued the old man, "all will be right now; the sahibs will slay the tiger, and once more the people can come back to their beautiful village." We agreed to make at least an attempt to kill the tiger, but were considerably handicap- ped by the lack of a guide who knew the ground where the tiger generally lay. The old man told us, however, that he was momentarily expecting a visit from his grandson, 'n,ho was to bring him some rice, and that the grandson could fetch some of the villagers to act as guides. Accordingly we, decided to remain in the village all night, and to start upon the tiger's trail in the morning. (to be Confirmed.)

• RAIN. Rain, rain, go away. come again another day ! " Oh, the dancing leaves are merry, And the blossMing grass is glad, But the river's to rough for the ferry, And the sky is low and sad. Yet the daisies shake with laughter As the surly wind goes by, For they know what is hurrying after, As they watch the dim, grey sky; The clovers are rosy with saying— (The buttercups bend to hear) "Oh, be patient, its only delaying— Be glad, for its very near!" The blushing pimpernel closes, It isn't because it grieves-- • And down in the garden, the roses Smile out from their lattice of leaves! Such gladness has stirred the flowers! Yet children only complain: "Oh. what is the use of showers?" "Oh, why does it ever rain?"

OFF TEE BEATEN TRACK,

MORLEAT ROBERTS (Author. " A 411odern „Avernus," p.).

I got to Terracina Jule at night one Jrnmary, and though I own that,thinge pest touch ine with no sense of sympathy as things yet to be, my heart beat a little fester as I drove in the darkness through , this ancient Anxur, once a stronghold of the Volseians. Here too I left the railway, and the southern road was befere Me. Terracina was touched with literary memories ; Washington Irving had written about that very same old inn at 'Fermenta to which I was going, that inn which poor de- ceived I3aedeker called Grand Hotel Royal in small capitals. I was among the Volacians in the Appian Way, in the country of brigands, with the spirit of Irving. And suddenly I drove across rough paving stones in the heavy shadows of vast corridors, and was greeted by a feeble and broken-down old landlord who wished the noblest Signor of them all, my undistinguished self, all good things. Poor Prancia was the very spirit of a deserted landlord. I imagined that he might have remembered prosperous days be- fore the railway through Monte Cassino and Sparanise robbed 'Fermenta of her robber's dues from south-bound travellers. His vast hotel, entered meanly by a little hall, was dimly lighted by candles. With another feeble crea- ture, once a man, be preceded ins, and speaking poor French, said he had my letter and had prepared me ths best apartment in his house. We climbed stone staircases as one might climb the pyramids, wandered on through resounding and ghostly corridors, and finally came to a room as vast as a quarry and ellmost as chilly as a catacomb. When he placed the candle LW a cold slab of a table and withdrew. with many bows, I could have imagined myself a lost apirit. There was just sufficient light to see the dark- ness. The room was a kind of tragedy in iteelf : the floor was stone: a little bed in one far dis- tant corner was only to be discovered by travel. It was a long walk to the window. Outside saw white foam breaking in the harbour now silted up and wholly useless.

===== 0002601_19060120_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
AN HOT= OF MAGNIFICMNT DISTANCES.

I dined that night in another hall which could have accommodated a hundred. I was lost in shadows. But then I was a shadow among shades. This WAS the past indeed, an ancient world. And after dinner at last I got a bath. it took me two hours to get it, and when it came it was nothing more than a great kettle for boiling fish in. I knew it was that by the smell. I rejected it for a bason which was al- most as large as an English saucer for a break- fast cup. And then I slept. I felt that I was in a tomb sleeping with my fathers. It was a kind of unexpected resurrection to wake and find daylight about me. I had meant to stay for a little while at Ter- racism, but somehow I took a kind of scun- ner ' at this poor old hotel of magnificent dis- tances and the lingering, doddering, unwashed old men who acted as chambermaids. Perhaps, too, the fish-kettle as a bath was u discourage- ment. No bath at all can be put up with in course of time, but a fish-kettle invited Inc to be clean and yet did not allow me to smell so. I went down to my prehistoric landlord and re- quested him to get me a carriage to go on to Formic, where I should be once more in touch with the rail. I instructed him to get it for me at a reasonable price, and that price I knew to be about 20 lire, or francs. For the first time in my Italian experience I had come across hotel keeper who was not in league with the owners of carriages. I was soon made aware of this by overhearing an awful uproar in the big outside corridor. I lighted a cigarette and went out to find the landlord and man of carriages, a very black and hairy brigand, enjoying them- selves as only southerners can when they are making a bargain or eonabinazione. The old landlord bnsked up wonderfully at the prospect of such a struggle. It doubtless reminded him of days long past. It made his sluggish blood flow. I believe that he would not have missed the excitement even to pocket etllarge commis- sion from his opponent. I was so rare a bird and he had nbt seen a traveller since heaven knows when. My Italian is poor, but I under- stood some of the uproar. The man of carriages presumed that I was a noble gentleman who de- sired the best and would be ready to pay for it. The landlord retorted that even if I was a prince and a millionaire, both of which seemed likely, it was uo reason I should be robbed. He sug- gested fifteen lire and the e outraged brigand shrieked and demanded forty. For an how they wrangled and haggled and swore. First one made believe to go and then the other. hbayvefraiicDTllloerye tclaniumeolnipce taulndy northernercamenwforiaillide anti- cipated bloodshed. They struggled and beat the pelnis of their hands with outstretched fingers. it took them half an hour to quarrel over the last two francs. And finally it was settled that the noble prince and millionaire then leaning against the wall smoking cigarettes was to pay 22 lire and to give a pourboire. They shook hands over it and beamed. My old land- lord wiped his brow and communicated the re- sult to me with tears of pride. I thanked him for his care of my interests and paid his modest bill at once. He entreated me to speak well of his hotel the Albergo Reale. And really I have done my best. ITALY, THE MODERN AND THE MEDIXYAL.

The brigand furnished me with a decent pair of horses, decent at any rate for Italy, and I left for Formia before noon. Now I was nu longer on the railway, but on the real road, the Appian Way, and I felt in a strange dream, such as might well come to one on a spot where ancient Rome, the age of the Goth, and the Mediteval Italy and modern times mingled. By the road were fragments of Roman tombs, at Torre dell' Epitafla was the ancient southern boundary of the Papal States, in reedy marshes by the road and near the sea were herds of huge black buffalo. And the sun shone very brightly for all that it was winter: the distances were fine blue ; the sea sparkled, and the earth even then showed its fertility. Eleven miles from Terraeina we drove bite Fondi. And the sky clouded over as indeed it should have done. For Fondi is a gloomy and unhappy, a sullen and unfortunate looking town; Once it uas a noted haunt of brigands, and even yet as the sullen peasants stand about its one great street, which is still the Appian Way, they looked as if they regretted not to be able to seize me and take me to the hills, to hold me to ransom. But Fondi, gloomiest of towns, has other stories than those of the brethren Era Diavolo. There is a Castle in the town, once the property of the Colonnas, and in the six- teenth century this palace was attacked by a pirate, Barbarossa, a Turk, and a daring one. His object was to capture Countess Giulia Gonzaga for the harem of the Sultan. He failed but played havoc among its inhabitants and burnt part of the town. It uas rebuilt and burnt again by the Turks in 1594. We rushed through the latter part of the gloomy town at a gallop. I was glad to see the last of it and get into the clear air. Then my horse climbed the long slope of the Monte S. Andrea, where the steep road is cut through hills, while I walked. And then as evening came on we swept down into Itri. This too was gloomy, but not, like Fondi, built upon a flat. This shadowy wreck of ancient times lies on hills and among them. It was an air of mountain savagery ; it looks like a ruined medireval fortress. Broken arches, once part of the Appian Way, are made into substructures for ragged. ruinous modern houses. The place is peaked and pined, desolate, hungry and savage. In it was born Fra Diavolo who was brigand, soldier, and political servant to Cardinal Ruff° when the French Republic in the Beginning of the 19th century invaded the Kingdom of Naples. Once he was lord of the country from the Garigliano to Porten° ; he even interrupted all communications between Naples and Rome. He was sentenced to death and a price put ou hie head. Finally he St as shot at Daronisei.

POILMIA. That night I slept in a little Italian .nn by the verge of the quiet sea. There also, KS at Terracina. ancient and doddering men acted as unumnerwatels. They wftnaerect irs With mat- tresses and sheets, until I wondered where the women were and what they did. And outside was a fountain whence Formia drew water, as it seemed, all the night, chattering of heaven knows what. For Formia is a busy and beauti- ful little town. On the north side it is sheltered by a high range of hills ; on the lower slopes are grown oranges and lemons and pome- granates ; there also are olive groves and vine- yards. I stayed a day in among the Formian folk and then Naples, which one can almost see from the terraces above the town, drew me south. At the Villa Caposele one can see Gaieta itself to the South and Ischia in the blue sea, Casamicciola facing one. I remember how the Italian nature came out when I arranged to go to the station to take the train for Sparanise. I had but little baggage and it was put on a truck for ins by the landlord of the Hotel dei }Uri. I walked on to the station and the boy who pulled the truck followed. As he came up the little slope to the station I saw that eight Of ten others were pretending to help him, and I knew that they would inevitably want some pence for assisting. In a few moments I was surrounded by the eager crowd. "Signor, I pushed behind !" " And, Signor, so did I." And, oh, but it was hard work, Signor !" And every one who could have had a finger on the little truck wanted his finger paid. They were insistent, clamourous, and at the same time curious to see how the stray foreigner would take it. I perceived a gleam of humour in them, and to their disappointment. yet to their immense delight, for the Italian admires a degree of shrewdness, I stared them all over and burst into laughter. They saw at once that the game was up, and they shrieked with laughter at their own discomfiture. I gave the boy with the truck his lira, dropped an extra ten centesimi into his palm, and said suddenly, " Scappate via " They gave one shout more of laughter and ran down the bill. And as for me, I got into the train cud went to old quarters of mine in Naples. lint I was glad to have been off the beaten track for once.

The scene outside the Town Hall was one which will live long in the memories of those who were privileged to witness it from the municipal build- ings. The spacious lawn in front of the Town Hall, and the carriage-ways on either side were densely packed with a crowd numbering many thousands,the tail of which extended across Sankey- street, As early as 7.45 the spectators began to assemble and even before that hour a number of enterprising juveniles entered upon a lengthy and more or less weary wait for the result to be declared. For a time comparative quiet prevailed but towards 8.30 party feeling began to manifest itself. Cheers and counter cheers were raised and good humoured pleasantries were indulged in. One of the watchers, a member of the gentler sex, had the temerity to appear on the scene in a striking blue hat which aroused the interest of those by whom she was sur- rounded. Had she remained quiescent all would have been well but in her excitement she removed her hat and commenced to wave it vigorously. In , a few minutes the headgear of which , she had been so proud presented a bedraggled app6arance, friend and foe alike helping in the work of destruction. Towards 9.30 the crowd began to show signs of impatience but they retained their good humour. The figures when known were exhibited in the window of the Borough Surveyor's office and the appearance of a gentleman in the room to see that everything was ready was the signal for a tremendous outburst of , cheering. Shortly afterwards the Mayor made his appearance, accompanied by Mr. Arthur Crosfield whose expression was that of the victor and not the vanquished. The approach of these gentlemen was the signal for a perfect tornado of cheering amidst which the Mayor announced the figures. Not a woi d of what he said could be heard. Curiosity as to the details of the victory was for the moment overcome by the desire to express wild delight at the return of the Progressive candidate. Comparative quietness was restored but there was another outburst of cheering as the figures announcing the overwhelming majority were displayed. A few Minutes before this some irrespon- sible individual set up a blue rocket and as this messenger mounted upwards it was accompanied with a chorus of vigorous " hoots " and "cat- -- calls." This blue rocket was to Majority Shortly before the counting con- cut of all as, ignorant of the cluded Nlr. Conservative friends as well as my Liberal friends. (Cheers.) It is a great happiness to me to know that during the great fight,though we have hit hard politically, I have not lost a single friend amongst my many Conservative acquaintances—(cheers)---and so while feeling has run so high I am glad that cheerful good temper has been even more marked in Warrington than it has in the rest of Lancashire. (Cheers.) Bravo Warrington! (Prolonged cheers.)

Like Alexander, I sigh for more worlds to conquer. (Cheers.) When I come to see you again I know I shall meet with your sympathy,and I hope often in the future to stand by the side of Mr. Crosfield. (Cheers.) I congratulate Warrington and my Irish friends for wiping out the insult to the late Mr. Crosfield, and returning his son. (Loud cheers). .

The speech-making had just con- cluded when the torch light procession headed by Messrs. Crosfield's band was seen approaching. At the Club the procession made a halt and the band played "Rule Britannia" and " See the conquering hero comes," the crowd cheering wildly.

SPEECHES IN THE CLUB.

Anticipating that Mr. Crosfield would , speak in the billiard room, several I hundreds of people assembled there and when the result of the poll became I known excited cheers were raised and I continued for many minutes. The arrival of the Rev.H. Dunnico and his taking up of a point of vantage on a billiard table was the signal for a fresh outburst andfor rupwards of five minutes the rev, gentle- Man strove in vain to obtain a hearing. His opening remarks were not well received as he had to announce that the member, who was addressing a huge ; crowd from one of the windows of the Club, would not be able to speak in the billiard room as it was impossible for him to get there, the corridor being so densely crowded. He was greeted with cries of "He must come," I" He will have to come," and a depu- tation at once made for the assembly .room to ask Mr. Crosfield to attend in the billiard room and if necessary to enforce his presence. The latter expedi- ent had not, however, to be resorted to as Mr. Crosfield cheerfully consented to listen to the wishes of his suppOrters,and he was carried in by his jubilant friends and placed on the billiard table along- side Mr. Dunnico. His appearance was the signal for an outburst of enthusiasm such as has never before been witnessed in the Club. Time after time he endeavoured to speak but his voice was drowned by the cheering, and it was only when Councillor Evans threatened that the member would leave the room without delivering an address that those present controlled their feelings and silence was restored. Prior to Mr. Crosfield's arrival speeches,punctuated freely with cheers, were delivered by several gentlemen. The Rev. H. DUNNICO said : This is the greatest Liberal victory which has ever taken place in Warrington. (Cheers). We have beaten the brewers. (Cheers). We have struck a great blow for Free Trade and have put an end to Chinese Slavery. (Cheers). You and I feel highly encouraged that War- rington has taken its stand on the side of the progressive constituencies of the country (Cheers). We believe Mr. Cros- field will go to support Sir Henry Cainpbell-Bannerman (cheers) and to help him to can-y into operation all those laws which will benefit the rank ' ! and file and the whole of the working- , , classes of this country. (Cheers). You ; ; have sent a man to represent you who ; is a sound temperance reformer-1 (cheers)—a man who will witness ; bravely for liberty of conscience, a man who will hear nothing of the evil attempt to tax the poor man's bread. (Cheers). We believe that this is not only a Liberal victory which is for this hour, but that having sent Mr. Croifield to represent us we will send him again , and again. (Prolonged cheers.) The Tories are down-hearted. They never: thought we could do this. (Cheers). i Councillor EVANS said : When Mr. Chamberlain made known what his future policy was the people of the I country remembered PeterloO, and ; made up their minds that they would I • not have a tax put upon the food of those who were weakest and least able I to bear the burden. (Cheers.) I think we can congratulate ourselves upon the ; splendid victory which we have won in ; in Warrington.. (Cheers.) The win means we have put another nail in the coffin which covers the corpse of Pro- tection—a corpse which we thought was dead and buried years ago, and never again to be resurrected—and it ; will be many many years before the Conservative party make another, attempt at the resurrection of Protec- tion, which means the taxation of the ; bread of our children and the levying of a tax upon those least able to bear the burden of paying it. (Cheers.) I think we *have every reason to be abundantly satisfied with the result of ; the election. (Cheers.) We have been well led, and the rank and file have; done their work well. (Cheers.) Miss CROSFIELD,said : We have had ; a splendid victory. • During the last ten days one's heart has been filled with enthusiasm at the splendid and unselfish work which has been done on all hands. (Cheers.) We have been good friends in the past and we will be firmer friends in the future—(cheers)— and if we stand shoulder to shoulder as we have done in this election we shall accomplish great things. (Cheers.) Mr. A. H. CRosFIELD, M.P., delivered a brief speech during which_ he said :

This is a moment of great encourage- ment to the progressive party in War- rington. It is a moment of high and well grounded hope for all those who in this strange and sad world are groping their way forward, with slow steps it may be, ' to nobler ideals and to a better kind of civiliza- tion in the future. (Cheers.) We have fought our little battle in good temper in Warrington. (A voice" Thb other side have not "). In England we ' are accustomed to fight our battles and give and take the cut and thrust of political warfare in better part than the people of any other country in the world,but we must not forget in our political hard fighting that there is something greater behind. (Cheers). We must not forget that politics are merged in true statesmanship and while you and I have great objects to strive for—to raise the people of this blessed island of ours—and that while true statemanship like charity begins at home it does not end there. (Cheers.) While our first duty is to try and erase the stain on the escutcheon of this old island of ours and to wipe off the black stain from the honoured name of England, we must yet be ready to hold out a helping hand to peoples less fortunate than ourselves and see that while you and I and all progressives are fighting to make Eng- land a still nobler example to the world we are also helping other peoples to follow in our footsteps. (Cheers.)

Mr. OWEN TooLE seconded. They were challenged, be said, as to certain leads which were given by Mr. Madden, and, he was proud to say, officially given by himself and Mr. Sexton on behalf of the Warrington Branch of the United Irish League. That day ',too Catholics had voted for Mr. Crosfield, while the number who voted for Mr. Pierpoint was under 50. (Cheers.) He was glad that the challenge had been. made, and was proud of the way it had been answered. (Cheers.) The vote was accorded with extreme heartiness.

Mr. Crosfield received another great ovation on leaving the club for the Patten Arms Hotel. Here he addressed another huge gathering which cheered him enthusiastically. The torch-light procession traversed the main streets of the town and scenes of great excite- ment continued until early morning. Warringtonians, it was evident, were over-joyed at having returned Mr. Crosfield to represent them in the ' British Parliament.

MR. CROSFIELD CONGRATU- LATED.

Mr. Crosfield has been the recipient of letters and telegrams of congratula- tion from all quarters, among those wiring being Earl Carrington. Earl Crewe, Sir John and Lady Brunner, the Right Hon. D. Lloyd-George, Mr. Whiteley, (Liberal Whip), Mr. W. H. Lever, Mr. and Mrs. Emmott Barlow.

Lives of great men all ramind us We can make our lives sublime, And departing leave behind us Footprints in the sands of time. Footprints that perhaps another, Sailing o'er life's troubled main, Some forlorn and weary brother Seeing may take heart again. (Cheers).

I hope that will be one of the mottoes on °kir political shield and that we will fight our battles in years to come, when progress in which civilization is assited by an advance in science and education, and by a broader and more practical kind of religion, and assisted by these great victories, so that we may make steps forward more rapidly than we have been able to do in the past, and that we will help not only the people of Britain but of the Emerald Isle. (Cheers.) I hope that the happier times for our people may in the future, and I hope the none too dis- tant future, be followed by somewhat happier times throughout the whole world. (Cheers.) Let me, having received your kind support, ask you to give three British cheers for the mag- nificent victory which we have won in Warrington. (Cheers).

LETTER FROM MR. R. PIERPOINT Mr. Crosfield has also received a letter from Mr. Robert Pierpoint congratu- lating him upon his success and wishing him happiness in his new sphere.

Mr. HOWES, in response to repeated calls, made a short speech. He said that when the fight was commenced they took as their motto "Work will win." They had worked hard and !victory was theirs. (Cheers.) Dur- ing the whole of his electioneer- ing experience he had never been associated with a more enthusiastic or earnest band of workers than in War- rington. (Cheers.), It would be invidi- ous to mention anyone by name for all had done their best. So far as War- rington was concerned he had taken off his armour and the next day he would be fighting in the cause of progress by the side of their friend Mr. Neilson-- (cheers)--and he hoped to have another victory there. (Cheers.)

Alderman TINNION said that first and foremost the victory was one for Free Trade but they had been helped by others. He must congratulate Mr. C.B. Parker on the splendid work which he had done as President of the Liberal Association—(cheers)--and he must not forget in the time of victory their Irish and Catholic friends who had stood splendidly by them. (Cheers.) The time would come when their Irish friends would ask their support in return and he was sure they would stand by them. (Cheers.) They had had the support of the Trade Unionists bf Warrington and they must stand by the Trade Unionists. (Cheers). They had won the seat and the victory should prepare them so that they would keep it and see that Warrington was never again represented by a Tory. (Cheers.)

Mr. MADDEN said the victory had been a great one for the Empire, for England, Ireland, Scotland and Wales, for Liberalism and for the Labour party who had been the backbone of the victory. (Cheers.) Warrington had wiped out the insult which it offered to Mr. John Crosfield by return- ing his noble son. (Cheers.) They had carried dismay into the Tory camp and their Tory friends who had existed for years on the spoils of office had gone home in dismay and with broken hearts. (Cheers.) Warrington belonged in the future to Liberalism and Labour and Irishmen would have their share also. (Cheers.)

" Mr. W. HAIGH paid a high tribute to the excellence of the work done by Mr. J. Howes, as Mr. Crosfield's election agent, Mr. Howes could not take a better testimonial with him to another constituency than Warrington's victory. He moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Howes for his services. (Cheers.)

MR. CROSFIELD'S POLITICAL ENGAGEMENTS. Mr. Crosfield has promised to address meetings at Bangor and Carnarvon this (Friday) evening on behalf of Mr. Lloyd George. On Saturday he will speak at Hoylake in support of Mr. W. H. Lever's candidature and on Monday he will address a meeting at Winnington or Sir J. T. Brunn er, Bart.

AT THE CONSERVATIVE CLUB. Mr. PIERPOINT addressed a crowd of ' his supporters from the balcony of the : Conservative Club immediately after the,declaration of the poll. He said he could not, of course, deny that he was exceedingly disappointed at their defeat, but he had not lost his courage at all. They were all liable to have their ups and downs, and the election so far as it had gone reminded him of the election of 1880. They knew per- fectly well that that disaster hacl-17-- repaired very well indeed. (Cheers.) This disaster would be repaired before very long. (Cheers.) He wished to be allowed, before he said good-night, to return thanks to all who had helped him at the poll that day. (Cheers.) A few minutes later Mr. PIERPOINT addressed an audience in the assembly room. He said he need scarcely state that they were all of them very much disappointed at the result of the poll; but the comfort they had was that they had done their best and that they had fought the election in a perfectly honourable and straightforward way.. I (Cheers.) He should have been delighted to have served them in the coming IParliament. but as it happened the electors of the town had willed it other-- wise. At all events he-could comfort his wife and she could comfort him by telling each other what they knew to, be the truth, that they had -both worked as hard as they could and had done their -best to win. So had their worthy friend Sir Gilbert Greenall. (Cheers). They would have to bear for awhile with the new Paha- ment. As far as one could judge it would be composed in the majority of their opponents. He did not say it would last very long. He had not lost his courage. (Cheers). He desired to thank all who had worked for him and for the cause, and more particularly Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall. (Loud cheers). Sir Gilbert had been abso- lutely devoted to the cause since the, election began. He thanked also his, old and good friends. Mr. John White and Mr. Draper, for the hard and assid- uous work they had done as well as the ladies for their absolute devotion to the cause they had so much at heart, (Cheers). He couln not particularize ' others, but he thanked all who had been 'of assistance. He also returned his. wife's thanks.

BRAVO, WARRINGTON! LIBERALS WIN AT LAST.

MR. A. H. CROSFIELD'S ENORMOUS MAJORITY,

The most momentous battle in the political history of our ancient borough was decided on Monday, and the electors gave their verdict overwhelm- ingly on the side of Free Trade and Progress. The most magnificent victory ever recorded since Warrington re- turned a representative to Parliament was achieved by Mr. A. H. Crosfield after the keenest fight waged in the constituency. The Liberals had all along worked with remarkable enthu- siasm inspired by a sure confidence that their cause would triumph,but cer- tainly none had been so sanguine as.to expect the record majority of 1.500. Warrington, however, has been pro- foundly moved by the efforts to under- mine our fiscal policy and has followed the splendid lead of Manchester and the other great northern industrial centres which have rejected "the raging and tearing propaganda" by great majori- ties. The progressives of Warrington have indeed full reason to congratulate themselves on this brilliant consumma- tion of their efforts, for after twenty- five years of Tory representation, the town has now conferred its greatest honour on a sound Liberal.

Although the election campaign was short, the work of educating the constituency had been in progress for many months. As the critical time drew nearer-the enthusiasm of the large band of workers who had rallied round the standard increased wonderfully. On the day of the poll the energies of the united forces were redoubled, and nothing was left to chance. The perfect organization which had developed under Mr. J. Howes' able direction worked smoothly and efficiently and was an all-important factor in bringing libout the ultimate triumph.

===== 0002601_19060120_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
THE POLL.

The poll was an exceedingly heavy one for 93 per cent of the electorate .exercised the franchise. The scenes at all the polling stations were animated in the extreme. School children had a holiday and naturally gravitated to the neighbourhood of the booths where they amused themselves aric-1 enlivened the proceedings at first by cheering all and sundry. Ultimately, however, party feeling asserted itself and almost every visitor was greeted with a mixed chorus of cheers and hoots from the rival factions. Many of the streets were quite gaily decorated. and in this direction plenty of orignality was shown. Big loaves suspended from banners dangled over the heads of pedestrians and there were cartoons and portraits galore. Party favours have never been more generally worn, and it was noticed that red easily predominated. Doers trotted about in all the glory of coloured ribbons denoting the politics of their masters.

Throughout the day workers were en- gaged in bringing electors to the poll and the exceptionally large percentage of voters is a tribute to the success of their efforts. The ladies were hardly one whit behind the men in their activity on behalf of the Liberal candi- date and all -ivoiked with really sur- prising energy. The number of vehicles brought into requisition surpassed all pievious records, and motor cars decorated with party colours sped hither and thither in all the wards. A visit to any of the polling stations dur- ing the day convinced one that the issue was never in doubt,for the returns at the Liberal Committee rooms were emi- nently satisfactory and the workers were in the highest spirits. The heavy rain which fell in the afternoon did not result in any diminished activity, and its effect on the poll must have been infinitesimal. Voting, of course, was heaviest during the dinner hour and in the evening and at those times excite- ment ran high. There was no lack of humorous incidents to provide diver- sion. In the Whitecross Ward a huge giraffe stalked about at leisure. Not even the children, however, were alarmed, for it was speedily dis- covered that this wonderful animal was akin to the species seen on the pantomime stage—in fact it was the ingenious device of two youths, one of whom wAs mounted on stilts. Young- sters formed themselves into proces- sions and went about giving vent to party war cries, and it was evident that they enjoyed the day more than anyone. During the morning Mr. Crosfield made the round of the wards, accom- panied by Miss Crosfield, and had stirring receptions. A large contingent of members of the Women's Liberal Association, followed in a stage coach. Mr. C. E. Parker. the president of the Liberal Association was. determined to leave no stone unturned to secure Mr. Crosfield's return, and he was here, there and everywhere, rendering invalu- able services.

THE REGISTER.

The number of persons entitled to vote was 10,365 and the following list gives the number in each ward. ' Polling Ward. No. of voters. Lodg- District. Div I. Div 2. Div 3. ers. 1 Town Hall 2 Whitecross 3 Bewsey (No. 1) 4 „ (No. 2) 5 Orford Winwick - 133 6 St. John's 1,561 6 156 7 Fairfield Ft Howley 1,009 16 119 1 0 St. Austin's (No. 1) 312 26 80 7 10 „ (No. 2) 667 1 42 11 Latchford (No. 1) 968 4 201 3 12 „ (No. 2) 369 2 54 1 Latchford Without 64 —4 SUMMARY No. on Municipal List, Divs. 1 and 3... 11,258 Parliamentary List Divs. 1 and 2, and Lodgers 10,365 COUNTING THE VOTES.

At eight o'clock the polling boothsl were closed, and the ballot boxes were conveyed with all speed to the Town Hall. In the Council Chamber a large , staff was in readiness to count the votes, and operations were soon corn--; menced in the presence of the Mayor (Alderman Bolton), the election agents (Messrs. J. Howes and E. Draper) and other prominent workers. There were some anxious moments, but in a short time it became evident that Mr. Cros- field had achieved the victory. When the extent of the majority was known the Liberals were naturally delighted, but there was, of course, no demonstra- tion.

THE RESULT. The Mayor declared the result as follows at about 9.40 : Arthur H. Crosfield. Robert Pierpoint

===== 0002601_19060120_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
MEMORABLE SCENES AT THE

LIBERAL CLUB.

Immediately after the declaration of the poll thousands of Mr. Crosfields supporters made their way to Bewsey- street and a never-to-be forgotten scene I was witnessed. The club itself was packed to overflowing while in the street an immense crowd awaited the arrival of the member. When Mr. Crosfield drove up in a cab cheer upon cheer was raised, the enthusiasm being intense. After a short interval Mr. Crosfield, Miss Crosfield, and several prominent workers were seen at the upper windows of the club and the reception they received was a really magnificent one. So delighted were the multitude at the glorious Liberal victory that they had a difficulty in restraining their cheers even while a few short speeches were made. Alderman PIERPOINT, who has taken a splendid part in the fight, said: What Manchester did on Saturday— (cheers)—Warrington has done to-day (Loud cheers). I have been waiting for twenty five years for an episode of this character. lam proud to-day of my native town—(cheers)—and I am proud of the voters who have vot ed in the manner they have done to-day(Cheer s) The majority is 1,500 (loud and pro- longed cheers)—a majority which will in a great itieasure help to encourage the fighters in other boroughs. It will give extreme pleasure to our good friend Mr. Lloyd George—(cheers)— and also to the labour party. We have now a Crosfield for our member— (cheers)—and we sincerely wish him many years of health and strength to carry on his efforts in the British House of Commons on behalf of the workers of this country (Cheers). Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD, M.P., said : On this momentous victory I beg, with your kind indulgence, to say a few words. Fellow-townsmen, one and all I thank you—(cheers)—and especially the strenuous workers who have helped to bring about this great victory. (Cheers.) Alderman Pierpoint has said ' that, like Manchester, Warrington has set a grand example to ,the country. (Cheers.) And might we not say in all honesty that you have set an example to the world? (Cheers.) At any rate, you have given England a lead that England may set an exarhple to the world which in future time will be followed. (Cheers.) You have decided this issue, partly lookinc, back at a record of Government which You considered to be bad—(hear, hear)—and more hopefully looking for- ward to the future so that we may do more than defend the citadel of Free Tra.de—(cheers)—and that with the strength of honest public opinion we ' may carry into effect those reforms v.rhich will raise our people to la higher level of civilization— (cheers)—and which will enable us to realize loftier ideals. (Cheers.) I can- not conclude without expressing the thanks of my sister and myself for the great kindness and generosity shown to us alike by our Liberal and Conserva- tive friends. (Cheers.) I thank you all from my heart—and particularly the zealous and enthusiastic workers, who with a fine public spirit and without other inducement have come forward a.nd won this glorious victory— for "Nvhat old Oliver Cromwell would have called a crowning mercy, in a political sense. Let us re- member all this with pride—that though political feeling has ru,n high dpring the last fortnight there never was an election fought with more cheerfulness and good temper. I am quite sure that all of us to-night, now that the fight is over, will be as good friends, whatever our opinions may be, as we were before, and that we will take care that there is not a single incident to mar the honourable record of the last fortnight. (Cheers.) Mr. C. E. PARKER, president of the Liberal Association, briefly addressed the crowd. He said : We have put Mr. Crosfield into Parliament. Let us keep him there. (Cheers.) This has been a soldiers'. battle, and the soldiers have won it for us. (Cheers.) Mr. WILLIAM MADDEN, who had a very hearty reception, said : This is -what Paddy gave the drum. (Cheers.) This is a great day for the empire, for England, for Ireland—(cheers)—for the Liberal party and for the Labour party, (Cheers.) The Greenalls, the Pierpoints. the landlords, and the classes are beaten. (Cheers.) The rats and the foxes are driven .to their holes. (Laughter.) Having won this fight. we are going to fight the county election and win that too. (Cheers.)

GENTLEMEN,— I desire to express my grateful and heartfelt thanks to the 5599 Electors who recorded their votes on Monday last in my favour. Our great victory is due to the enthusiastic and com- bined efforts of every section of the Progressive forces. whose work, aided by that of many Unionist Free Traders, has enabled ns to do more than our share in defeating the retrograde proposals of the Party of Pro- tection,iland in maintaining for our people unimpaired the priceless blessing of untaxed bread. lam especially grateful to all the &Seers and mem- bers of the various committees who have worked so arduously on behalf of our great cause. In my capacity as the Member for the Borough I shall do whatever lies in my power to promote the bast , Interests of my constituents without regard to either religious or political opinion. I beg to remain, gentlemen. Your obedient servant. A. H. CROSPIELD.

PADGATE WESLEYANISM. ----- Congregational Tea. Success attended the proceedings at the annual tea and entertainment in connection with the Padgate Wesleyan Sunday School which was held on Thursday evening of last week. A substantal tea to which over one hundred sat down, was provided, the tables being presided over by lady members of the congrega- tion. The room was crowded for the subsequent pro- ceedings, and had been tastefully decorated for the occasion by the Misses Richardson, NVard, Eaglesfield and Thornton. Mr. Frank Harris presided, and was supported by the Rev. Win. Mellor, Mr. H. Ward and Mr. F. Wright (superintendents), and Mr. J. E. Richardson. THE REPORT. Mr. J. Jackson presented his report which was of a satisfactory character. During the year he said, there had been a further increase in their membership. Owing to the increase of scholars, they were in urgent need of a larger assembly room, and they trusted that before long they would be able to provide the necessary accommodation. The average attendance throughout the year had been 77 in the morning and 163 in the after- noon. They had a staff of 27 officers and teachers. I he savings club had been very successful, and was now under the management of Mr. L. Bennett and Mr. H. Brough. There were 54 members who had contributed £llB 17s. 4d., which was an increase of £lO. At present the library was closed, but would shortly be re-opened with an addition of 80 new books. The treasurer com- menced the past year with a balance in hand of 18s. 2d!. The receipts amounted to £42 10s. 10'.d., and the expenditure £42 -Is. Gd. The officers appointed during the year were :—Superintendents, Mr. H. Ward and Mr. F. Wright, treasurer, Mr. James Gandy ; auditor, Mr. Percy H. Bennett : secretaries, Mr. G. H. White and Mr. John Jackson savings club treasurer, Mr. Leonard Bennett ; and librarian, Mr. H. Brough. The entertainment was an enjoyable one. A piano- forte solo was given by Mr. Hugh Mellor, and recita- tions by Miss A. Foyer, Miss Dawsou, Fred Newbold and Ernest Monks. sirs. James Bennett was very successful with two Irish songs, while Mr. Levi Richard- son was well received for his rendering of " Angels guard thee." A duet by Mr. Richardson and Mrs. Cottrell, entitled " Where violets grow." was rendered inn very pleasing manner, and Mr. Walter Brough met with a hearty reception with two violin solos. The Warrington Prize Quartet Party were in attendance, and their contributions were the chief features of the programme. Action songs were also given by several of the scholars, the children singing remarkably well. , The accomptnists were Miss C. J. Bennett, Miss Ward, and Miss E. Wilkes. A vote of thanks to all who had assisted to make the ' evening a success concluded the proceedings. WARRINGTON DRUIDS' LITIGATION. Alteration of Constitution. , In the Chancery Division, Mr. Justice Farwell bad opened before him on Tuesday the action of Houghton v. Bibby, by which certain members of the Warrington District Society of Druids Family Funeral Fund sought to restrain the president, secretary, treasurer, and trustees, as representing the society, from applying the fund of the society in a manner alleged to be ultra vi res. Mr. Fuller, in opening the case, said that the plaintiffs were mostly infants represented by their father, James , Houghton, who had been made a plaintiff, as under the; rules he was entitled to attend the meetings of ther society. The plaintiffs claimed an injunction to restrain the society front dealing with the funds other than in accordance with the society's constitution, from exclud- ling the plaintiff, James Houghton, from attending the meetings, and from carryiug on a life insurance business contrary to the rules, and for consequential relief, and a declaration that the defendants were liable for the funds that had been wrongfully employed so I far' and for a return to the society of Isuch kinds. The society was an unregistered Isociety, and though it bore the name of a well- known friendly society, the Ancient Order of Druids, it had no actual connection in constitution with that society, but hitherto all the members had been children of members of the Order of Druids, and the committee of management had been members of that order. ' Originally the society was merely confined to a district and the members paid their subscriptions either to a person appointed to collect them or at the offices of the 'society. It was not what was known as a collecting society. In 11103, however, it was changed from the first class of society into a collecting society, and its business to life insurance, and the matter was one of considerable importance, for unregistered societies of the first class were subject only to such obligations as were imposed by the Friendly Societies Act of 1896,and those of the second were subject also to the Industrial Insurance '2ompanies Act. There was an accuratilation of funds amounting to about £l,OOO, and it was feared that the change, which would involve very heavy ex- penses, would have the result of dissipating that accumulation. Mr. Jenkins, K.C., representing the defendants, did not deny that the society could not anything outside the rules without altering those rules, but contended that it could alter the rules. His Lordship came to the conclusion that no proper notice had been given the members of the proposed alterations of the rules. He accordingly made declaration that the rules, as altered ought not to be acted upon, and that the society had no power to con- duct an insurance business except for the purpose of providing decent interment for its members. Mr. Jenkins said that the rules would have to be rec..st and properly submitted to the members. His Lordship did not think it necessary to direct any accounts and inquiries, but ordered the defendants to pay the costs of the action.

Local Embezzlement Case. At Liverpool Quarter Sessions on Tuesday, John Beaumont Weatherall (31), shop manager, was indicted for having, at Warrington, between October and November, embezzled certain sums amounting to £3O, the moneys of his employer, John Snowden. It was stated that the prisoner managed .a clothier's shop in Warrington, and failed to account for the money. The jury found him guilty, and be was sent to gaol for three months.
